Security concerns on the internet have long been an issue, in part because the openness of the architecture makes it vulnerable. The security problems break down into several types, of which financial fraud and personal harassment are notable examples. The internet has instituted a few technical measures to address security concerns, including HTTPS, which Netscape created in 1994. Google’s Chrome browser began to label sites that did not use HTTPS as a potential security risk after 2018 (Wikipedia, 2025i). The security mechanisms are relatively weak: both HTTP and HTTPS depend on using the right port and a certificate from a trusted authority, and those certificates can be revoked, which has led to a need to check whether a certificate remains valid. The so-called ‘dark web’ (based on the Tor browser) offers a space for illegal activity because it is not traced.

Banks and other financial institutions are often targets, despite security improvements. Financial institutions and their customers have coped by requiring more passwords and two-factor authentication. In the days before internet banking, people went to a local bank to identify themselves in order to get cash. That was reasonably secure. For someone who wanted to pay bills by cheque, a signature represented a standard form of security, even though signatures are easily forged. Nonetheless, signature-based identification was so widely established in some cultures that countries like the USA still use signed paper cheques, even though this is slowly dying out in favour of electronic transfers.

In the mid-20th century a European cheque card allowed individuals to write special cheques to withdraw money from banks anywhere in Europe. The cheque card was essentially an ID-card from a bank, and itself had increasing layers of security as time went on. Various forms of traveller’s cheques served a similar purpose for people on trips. A ‘counter’ signature matching the original was necessary, plus often another form of identification such as a passport. The companies that issued the traveller’s cheques took no risks. They received the money first, and only guaranteed that the money was available. This form of pre-internet money transfer was cumbersome, but at some level reasonably secure. The concern remains that electronic transfer enables money transfers on a larger and less controlled scale. A password or PIN (personal identification number) has largely replaced signatures, and the possession of a bank-issued card is essential.

A range of forms of financial fraud occur on the internet, including ‘phishing’ (attempts to fool people into revealing sensitive information or installing malware) and direct attacks on an organisation’s software, either to steal directly or to hold their sites hostage until they pay the attackers. Governments and institutions do what they can to avoid these kinds of risks, but this can be hard in a context where the attackers have sufficient resources from hostile countries or organised crime. Denial-of-service attacks are one of the actions that hackers have used against all forms of businesses. These attacks come in the form of message ‘blasts’ so intense that normal customers cannot reach the site. Often these are short term, but nonetheless effective. Some countries avoid these problems by close supervision of who can be active on their networks. This is a strategy that appears to work, but is criticised as a form of censorship.

Political concerns

A relatively uncensored and unrestricted internet, as is typical in Western countries, easily becomes a platform for political demagoguery and all forms of misinformation. The same has historically been true for gossip and tabloid newspapers, with the important difference that gossip and tabloids had a limited local scope, while the internet can broadcast worldwide. Scientific misinformation represents a particularly challenging issue for information science because it undermines the public trust in science and scholarship. This will be discussed in greater detail in later chapters.

Political misinformation is rife on the internet, but its nature varies widely and never was restricted only to the internet. In Germany, the Nazis used radio broadcasts to attack the Weimar government, and World War 2 propagandists in the USA suggested that anyone with Japanese heritage might be a spy. Facts and a balanced view of the truth faced difficult challenges, probably because such arguments did not appeal to the emotions and fears of the wartime public. What the internet has enabled in recent years is for essentially any person with strong opinions to broadcast them beyond their circle of immediate friends. Professional politicians routinely take advantage of the internet’s openness to make their messages available, as do their opponents.

For a political message on the internet to be effective today typically requires more than words. Political websites offer full multimedia coverage. Advertisements in social media make regular use of images and videos as well, though sound is typically optional, since it becomes an unwelcome interruption. Those who want to get the latest opinions from their preferred candidate or party can go to the candidate’s website. Increasingly, news programmes on the internet also have distinct political viewpoints, as was previously the case with newspapers and magazines. From an information science viewpoint, nothing fundamental has changed except the ubiquity of access and the intensity of the multimedia associated with the viewpoints. Nonetheless, intensity matters for hotly contested issues. In many Western countries a push for ‘balance’ has replaced the more abstract goal of ‘fairness’, perhaps because what seems fair to one side seems unfair to another. Balance tries to present all sides equally. In practice this means that the editorial judgement that used to set standards for requiring and presenting facts increasingly allows all sides to choose their own version of the truth.

Conclusion

Internet technology serves as a communication tool whose primary purpose, like that of printing or broadcasting, is to share new information. The internet has changed how to seek and distribute information, and it has radically changed the speed with which that happens. In a real sense the internet has democratised the capacity to communicate with large numbers of people. It has opened access to, for example, all members of organisations like the iSchools or ASIS&T, or to anyone searching for information in publicly available blogs or posts on widely read social media. The transformative element of internet access is that it has reshaped the context in which people seek and use information. In pre-modern times the typical context was hardly more than a few hundred people, often near neighbours whom they had known all their lives. It is only a modest exaggeration to say that the internet has changed the information context to become nearly planetwide, at least for those privileged enough to have the tools necessary for internet access.

CHAPTER 8

Information Integrity

What is information integrity?

Information scientists need to care about the integrity of information, which is not always easy. Integrity standards measure how truthful and reliable information is according to contemporary understanding. Since the standards change over time, the perception of integrity can vary, and often has over the centuries. That does not mean that information integrity lacks enduring value, only that the context so greatly affects the understanding of integrity that it may seem to change over time. There are plenty of examples even in relatively modern times. For example, it was not until well into the twentieth century that most people in the Western world accepted the factual evidence that humans evolved over time and were not created in exactly their current form.

The challenge in establishing information integrity goes back to the old problem of sources of authority, which shift over time. An even greater problem has been how to interpret the relevance of a particular authority for a particular kind of information. People commonly claim that they believe what they see, but sight can be deceptive. Size, for example, is hard to judge at a distance, and colours appear different in different forms of light. It is possible to set standards for information integrity within a context where most people can agree, but even agreeing depends on nuances of language and means of measurement. Precision is also not the same in all languages or places. A rough sea, for example, has a different meaning for a person who easily becomes seasick than for someone already accustomed to the waves. Information integrity has a political component too, when it involves making harmful accusations. The statement that someone cheated on their taxes could depend on how strictly the person understands the tax code, which is itself so complex that even experts may not always agree. The value of a claim that someone cheated may vary with the context. In a country where cheating on tax is common, it may not matter.

Problems with information integrity are generally complex because of the many possible variations of meanings and measurements and consequences. Information integrity is hard to enforce, even in the scholarly world, where the topic is often discussed. The sections below will address some specific issues.

Information integrity in the past

While humans have probably always disagreed, the disagreement only becomes an integrity issue when two sides disagree about the factual basis. The Reformation era in Western Europe provides a historical example. Topics like ‘salvation’ mattered intensely for scholars and ordinary people. A factual question arose from the sale of ‘indulgences’, which infuriated the early Protestants, who felt that the mechanism for forgiving sins by paying for prayers to be said and for absolution lacked the integrity of true biblical authority. It was as if a physicist today suddenly doubted basics like the law of entropy, or a 21st-century chemist claimed it was possible to change base metals into gold. Doubting the information integrity of one established ‘fact’ can also call others into question.

Natural scientists have often pushed against the established rules of their time. In the 19th century both the biologist Charles Darwin and the geologist Charles Lyell used physical evidence to support their arguments for why the planet is older than everyone had believed on the basis of the biblical Creation story. This in a real sense was not simply heresy but seemed absurd to people who had grown up in a world in which biblical authority had primacy. In practical terms, their evidence seemed unbelievable. From an information scientist’s perspective, integrity problems arise when the consensus about relevant ways of gathering and applying evidence breaks down. This is a matter not just of change, but of changing assumptions that are so deeply ingrained in the culture that rational discussion becomes impossible. The facts of one side do not count as facts for the other. That is when accusations begin.

The more complex theories about the physical world become, the less that non-scientists play a role in the discussions simply because most ordinary citizens find formulations like E = MC2 too abstract to discuss, even though that is the simple form of Einstein’s more general formula: E = (MC2) / V2/c2. And, of course, special relativity is a simpler version than general relativity. Few people attend schools where they must learn formulas like this. Today few grow up learning complex arguments involving biblical literacy. Education matters for how people judge integrity issues, which is unsurprising, because most integrity issues are complex.

The public focus on academic integrity issues in recent decades has become especially concerned with issues about copyright and plagiarism. Copyright laws themselves have changed significantly over the last three centuries since the British Statute of Anne in 1710 first established a model of basic protections for authors. In the 19th century Charles Dickens famously complained about American publishers stealing his works (Hudon, 1964). Dickens lived on the money from the sales of his books, and the rights issues were financially important to him. His novels were popular in the USA, but he did not profit directly since the USA never joined the Berne Convention for the Protection of Literary and Artistic Works until as late as 1989. From an information science perspective, an excessive focus on plagiarism distracts from other more important issues, since plagiarism itself does not invalidate the truth of scholarly evidence, only its economic value. Plagiarism is mainly an ethical issue, not one of scientific accuracy. Plagiarism was rarely an issue in the paper-based world, because small amounts of plagiarism were difficult to detect until new digital tools became available that could check digital documents for plagiarism at the word level.

The best of these plagiarism checkers allow operators to specify how many consecutive words are counted as plagiarism. Some can also leave out ‘function’ words that carry no meaning and only make an English sentence more comprehensible. A weakness in some plagiarism software is a failure to recognise well known facts and scientific phrases, including those involving standard ways of describing statistical results. A problem is a lack of clarity in plagiarism standards. There are also cases where the plagiarism becomes so extreme as to devalue the scholarship. German Defence Minister Karl-Theodor zu Guttenberg’s doctoral dissertation contained so much plagiarism that it cost him both his doctorate and his political office (Pidd, 2011).

Many serious information integrity cases today involve some form of data falsification, which invalidates the results. A case that received a lot of attention during the COVID-19 pandemic and led to retractions in two highly respected journals, The Lancet and The New England Journal of Medicine, came about because a company called Surgisphere provided data that looked increasingly suspicious, and because the company itself ultimately declined to provide the whole of the data to the authors (Davey, Kirchgaessner and Boseley, 2020).

Ivan Oransky’s Retraction Watch blog and his database documents cases of data fraud going back at least to 2010. There are many reasons why research data can have problems, including ordinary human error, and the more complex a study becomes, and the more time pressure authors are under, the more information integrity problems grow. It is easy to blame the researchers, and certainly every scholarly researcher should try to avoid error, but governments and universities are also to blame for the pressure they put on scholars to publish. Hanson et al. (2023) wrote about ‘The Strain on Scientific Publishing’. The article addresses a well established concern and provides convincing data about the problem. Their concern is that the total number of papers submitted to journals has increased to the point that peer-reviewing is struggling to keep up, which means that problematic papers inevitably slip through.

Discovering integrity problems

Discovering information integrity problems represents an important task for information scientists, since false information undermines the public credibility of scientific and scholarly work. The task is hard because the ability to recognise problems and to prove them depends, as always, on the context.

Integrity problems in an area like medicine often also involve economic issues. For example, developing new medicines is expensive, and there is always pressure inside a company to show the medicine’s effectiveness. Since the pharmaceutical industry funds research at universities, their support also affects the willingness of funded scholars to look for unfavourable results. Professor Lisa Bero (2019) writes: ‘Industry funders can go to great lengths to suppress the findings of academic research when it’s not favourable to the company …’.

The amount of money needed to do serious research in medical fields inevitably plays a role, but it is not the only factor. The training of the people who do that kind of research is highly specialised and expensive. In practical terms, researchers often come from high-ranked universities that may have had corporate funding for their training facilities. As a result, the personal and institutional desire to represent funded research results as valid is hard to ignore. This can also mean that the desire to test the integrity of potentially questionable scientific results may be limited.

Integrity problems in medicine get more press coverage than those in other areas. Integrity problems in medicine may be easier to spot because of their effect on people, but historically this has not always been simple. At one time research on heart disease focused primarily on men, on the assumption that men were more vulnerable. ‘Heart disease was considered a man’s disease. … That attitude persisted throughout the 20th century, a time when questions about a woman’s health were centered on the parts of her body under her bikini, said Dr Gina Lundberg, clinical director of the Emory Women’s Heart Center and a professor at Emory University School of Medicine in Atlanta’ (Williamson, L., 2024).

This attitude counts as an information integrity problem because it suppressed relevant information about half of the human population, and such gaps could count as falsification. It is not quite fair to say that scholars deliberately ignored women’s health. In the context of the time, women’s ‘problems’ often seemed embarrassing to both sexes. Even in the mid-20th century one form of typical medical advice for gynaecological problems was for a woman to get married and have children.

While women cannot count as a minority, many minorities faced the same gap in health research. It is now well known that sickle cell disease especially affects people of African or Mediterranean heritage. The disease is deadly, and discoverable with appropriate testing. ‘Graham notes that the cause of sickle cell anemia has been known since the 1950s. But for many generations, he says, sickle cell anemia has not had the funding and research attention it deserves’ (DeNoon, 2005).

Less well known are other genetic diseases that affect Black Americans, including diabetes (60% more common in Black than white Americans), asthma (3 times as likely), lung scarring (15 times more common), lung cancer (50% more likely), strokes (twice the risk) and cancer (Black men have a 40% higher death rate than white men) (DeNoon, 2005). The point is that information integrity is not just about the mistakes that researchers make, but about information that no one had or had tried to discover.

Information integrity problems of every sort are hard to identify without being so directly involved in the topic that the errors stand out; and in a world where the research topics are highly complex, even those looking for problems may be concerned whether their own doubts could be errors. A cautious researcher might reasonably hesitate before accusing colleagues of integrity violations, even when those errors do not result from any fraudulent intention. A further problem is that the people who are responsible for quality in large labs frequently have the all-too-human problem of too little time to check everything.

Deliberate fraud is probably less frequent than ordinary human error, but it happens. Diederik Stapel was a successful psychology researcher in the Netherlands who was caught and ultimately admitted to making up the data in his papers (see also below). Stapel was not the only notable person who committed deliberate fraud: ‘The once-celebrated South Korean stem-cell researcher Hwang Woo Suk stunned scientists in his field a few years ago after it was discovered that almost all of the work for which he was known was fraudulent’ (Bhattacharjee, 2013).

Whose job?

The people officially responsible for finding research integrity problems are often journal editors, journal reviewers and dissertation supervisors, but that is generally secondary to evaluating the overall quality of a work. Integrity problems certainly affect overall quality, but actually finding and recognising such problems goes beyond what most can do. A particularly obvious error may stand out, or a reviewer may simply recognise passages from the scholarly literature. A person well trained in statistics should be able to recognise cases where an author may have violated the assumptions behind a statistical test, and reviewers can in theory run the data through their own analysis, as long as the data are available. Variance in results does not necessarily imply bad intentions or actual errors, since data are complex, though odd results certainly ought to trigger suspicion.

Such testing takes both time and expertise, and not all reviewers have either the expertise or the time. It is easy to say that journals should always pick reviewers who can do both, but the reality is (and probably always has been) that appropriate reviewers are hard to find and that they often feel so stretched that they simply lack the time to pursue problems that are not immediately obvious. In a sense, the role of reviewers may have become to serve as scapegoats. Most journals and most institutions provide too little time and resources for systematic investigation. Finding research integrity problems will also not enhance career opportunities, except occasionally when hunting for plagiarism in the writings of well-known persons, where software can do most of the work.

Doctoral dissertation advisors ought to understand the work that their students are doing, and most try to catch and correct any errors they see. Context matters here too. Advisors have a social relationship with their doctoral candidates from years of working with them, and a natural urge to protect them can outweigh suspicions. This does not mean that advisors willingly overlook problems, only that the urge to hunt for weaknesses may be weak. Other factors play a role too. Doctoral students are supposed to break new ground. In social science and humanities fields this can involve languages that the advisor may not know or knows imperfectly. While dissertation advisors ought to theoretically have access to all of a candidate’s data, there are increasingly cases where the data may involve confidential information, and both the law and institutional practice may offer incomplete guidance about appropriate access. A more common problem is that advisors simply do not have the time (or do not take the time) to review all of the data or to reanalyse all of the results. It could seem like a failure of trust if the advisor insisted on redoing all of the analysis, and could even be seen as giving the doctoral candidate too much help. The norm today varies enormously from discipline to discipline, but in most fields today, redoing all of the data and the whole of the analysis is uncommon.

No one advisor can grant a doctorate today at most institutions, since they require either second readers or outside reviewers to guarantee quality. A second reader often serves also in an advisory capacity for the dissertation writer, and may have important social connections. It is rare that a second reader is as involved with the research and writing. A second reader may also feel less obligation to spend a lot of time re-examining the analysis. The goal of involving reviewers from outside of the department or the university is to get a truly objective opinion. The principle makes sense, but the fact is that the interconnections among people in the same discipline means that no one with the skills to evaluate a dissertation lies completely outside of the social context of the advisor or doctoral candidate. Getting someone totally and completely from the outside risks involving a person who does not really understand the research methods or goals. Complete outside objectivity represents a mythical and probably unrealistic ideal. In general, dissertations are not a major source of integrity problems, perhaps because the rewards of getting the degree are not worth the trouble or the risk. The positive aspect is that the research community cares enough about information integrity that most doctoral dissertations include no deliberate fraud.

Master’s theses and works by bachelor’s students rarely get the same degree of attention. In many European countries and some Asian countries a master’s student must write a serious thesis and must make an oral defence. This is less common in the USA, where some schools do not even require a written thesis. The problem is that programmes with a large number of students lack the resources to handle substantial master’s or bachelor’s theses, and future employers may show little interest in a written thesis.

Students almost never accuse their professors of violating research integrity, except in very rare cases, such as happened to Leiden University psychology professor Diederik Stapel, when two doctoral students found the courage to go to their department head, who was himself a close friend of Stapel, and accused Stapel of research fraud. (Bhattacharjee, 2013). The problem was that Stapel made up data. His ‘explanation that editors wanted simplicity and elegance likely has an element of truth, but is also self-serving in shifting responsibility. He had enough experience with real data to be able to create a very plausible fictional account of the data gathering with situations and stories so plausible that there was no incentive to investigate too closely’ (Seadle, 2017). Stapel’s error was that he made mistakes about details. When he revisited the location where one experiment supposedly took place, the configuration of the chairs and other details simply did not match. Stapel admitted his fraud more readily than many.

Another scholar who apparently manufactured data was James Hunton, an accounting professor at Bentley University in Boston. The original problem that created suspicion appeared to be a simple mistake in the number of US offices a company had. Hunton used confidentiality agreements as the reason why he could not make the data available. When he could not provide convincing versions of the confidential agreements, suspicion grew. Hunton deleted all of the data on his work computer, which seems to have belonged to the university, and when the university hired experts they discovered a deliberate cover-up using an ‘eraser utility’ that had ‘run multiple times in an attempt (only partially successful) to delete all of the files on the laptop – including the metadata manipulation utility’ (Malone, 2014). Hunton vanished without a trace, and left behind many embarrassed colleagues, with whom he had shared the false data.

For the information scientist it is striking how difficult the discovery of integrity problems is, which means that chance plays a role in the discovery and makes it more likely that more undiscovered cases exist. Ivan Oransky’s Retraction Watch blog documents many of the cases that come to light, and performs an invaluable service, but his function is to document known problems rather than to discover new ones.

Measuring information integrity

Not all integrity problems are alike in the amount of damage they do to the scholarly and scientific community. Two very different measurement problems arise: those that involve only ethical issues and those that change the substance of results. Not every integrity issue sorts perfectly into one of these categories, and they are unequal in their effect and the kind of long-term impact.

Plagiarism is only one form of ethical problem, and ought in a sense to be the easiest to measure by counting the number of duplicate words. The US government Office of Research Integrity (ORI) claims: ‘As a general working definition, ORI considers plagiarism to include both the theft or misappropriation of intellectual property and the substantial unattributed textual copying of another’s work. It does not include authorship or credit disputes’ (ORI, 1994).

This is a common attitude but lacks nuanced measurement. A search for rules about exactly how many consecutive words count as plagiarism results in widely varying answers. One site that appears on a Google search suggests that more than three consecutive words (not counting function words) could be plagiarism. Under that rule, the phrase ‘three consecutive words’ would count as plagiarism. Other sites suggest five words, which runs into the same problem (Sefton, 2013).

In fact most sophisticated academic sites realise that the number of words matters less than the substance and originality of what an author is saying. King’s College London makes students send their essays through the plagiarism checker Turnitin before submitting them, and it warns students that the ‘plagiarism score will never be 0% and you should not aim for this. There will always be coincidental matches between your work and other submissions, and also “false” readings on quotations, footnotes etc.’ (King’s College London, 2016).

The VroniPlag website sets a standard that a work is suspicious when 10% of the pages contain text overlap. This only means that one or more phrases within the page appear to duplicate other works, not the whole page. For VroniPlag a significant issue is the reference: for them, an imperfect or distant reference can count as plagiarism. Under rules of this sort plagiarism is widespread. Stefan Senders (2008) takes a broader view of student plagiarism: ‘Only by analyzing the way the relationship of plagiarism takes shape can we say whether it is best fit by paradigms of “theft,” “fraud,” “infidelity,” or even excessive intimacy.’

Insofar as ‘excessive intimacy’ means an excellent memory, many established writers must plead guilty. Measuring plagiarism is as hard as defining it precisely.

Privacy has become an ethical problem that is less prominent than plagiarism, but violating confidentiality and privacy is a serious legal issue in many countries, especially within the European Union. It is as important to keep survey-type data confidential as interview data, and any data that someone could trace back to an individual respondent could be problematic. The issue is especially important with medical data. The reverse problem with privacy is the need for transparency about where the data came from and what they mean. Context matters greatly with all forms of data, and if a scholar so completely eliminates all context information that the background becomes obscure, that can also damage the integrity of the data. There is no easy solution other than being aware of the problem. When the options for discussing the context are clearly labelled, and when the reason is legal compliance, the ethical concern perhaps vanishes, but some unmeasurable uncertainty about the validity of the data can nonetheless remain.

Methodology represents another potential integrity problem. A public example comes from a paper where the authors needed additional data points and filled in the data using ‘Excel’s autofill function’ on the argument that ‘[i]f we do not use imputation, such data is [sic] almost useless’ (Smith, 2024). There are cases where the autofill function makes sense, but such data is merely an extrapolation, not original data, and should at the least be clearly designated as such. A more common problem is a broadcast survey where the researcher cannot know whether those answering are representative of the population. One common goal of a survey is to include as many respondents as possible to increase its reliability, but numbers alone do not guarantee reliability. Large numbers can skew the results if only an unrepresentative minority have answered. On 24 May 2024 Nate Cohn published an article in the New York Times in which he discusses why Donald Trump had appeared to have a lead in the polls for eight months. The problem was who, exactly, was responding to the polls. The answer appeared to be ‘disengaged voters’ who ‘are far likelier to report getting their news from social media’ (Cohn, 2024). If only a subset of people respond to a questionnaire, then the question becomes: how representative is that subset? The answer may be largely unknowable.

Information integrity is too dependent on context to be readily measurable, but falsification problems often come up in the literature, especially when young scholars feel so under pressure to publish that they take short cuts. Measuring integrity might ideally have an absolute scale, but the world is not so simple. There are small errors that may not really matter, and, historically, even Nobel prize winners make mistakes, such as when Fritz Haber based his theory about recovering gold from sea water on flawed data (see Chapter 2, section on ‘Reliable information’). No researcher is perfect, and most attempt to get reliable results. What the scholarly world could ideally do is to ease some of the pressure on scholars young and old to produce a set number of published works per year. To some degree the pressure is social and internal, but institutions could also put less stress on short-term than on long-term results. Changes in the incentive system are needed, and that requires institutional political choices that are never quick.

Assuring information integrity

Assuring information integrity is particularly important for information scientists, and ought perhaps to be a core focus of information science research. Unfortunately it is also an area where information scientists generally lack training beyond general admonitions to be honest. Integrity problems in the historical past were often associated with mistaken assumptions about what sources counted as reliable, or with economic hopes for profiting from a solution that seemed plausible, even if it did not work reliably. Results based on information lacking integrity often failed in the marketplace, simply because widespread testing over time revealed flaws. The same problem happens in modern times as well. In the long run, solutions based on flawed results will fail and will not reward their creators.

This is not to say that an open market for testing new ideas necessarily guarantees quality information. Darwin’s theories did not find acceptance everywhere. One of the alternatives came from Lamarck at the beginning of the 19th century, whose ideas about the ‘inheritance of acquired characteristics’ offered an alternative mechanism that seemed plausible for many years (Wikipedia, 2025j). Lamarckism found support in the Soviet Union under Joseph Stalin, but once the Soviet Union came to an end, support for Lamarck ended as well. Lamarckism had an unusually long lifetime for a theory where the evidence remained unconvincing for most scientists, but attempts to resurrect it persisted nonetheless into the later part of the 20th century.

There are plenty of other examples of theories that failed more quickly, such as the cold fusion announcement in 1989 – that other scientists could not replicate. These failed theories sometimes inspire fresh attempts to reach the same results using different means, as in this case, since the social and economic desire to make nuclear fusion into a viable energy source remains strong. In 2023 scientists in California claimed success by ‘shooting nearly 200 lasers at a cylinder holding a fuel capsule the size of a peppercorn … Now they say they have successfully replicated ignition at least three times this year …’ (Paddison, 2023). The process produced only energy ‘enough to boil around 10 kettles of water. But it was sufficient to make it a successful ignition and to prove that laser fusion could create energy’ (Paddison, 2023).

Physics is an area where public testing can effectively discredit false theories. The marketplace for disproving theories functions less well in other fields. Medicine is an area where integrity matters to people’s lives, but medical testing can also fail to catch problems, in part because the process of testing new medicines is complex and expensive, and in part because medical testing does not and perhaps cannot take into account all of the contextual factors that matter. A bigger problem is that ordinary citizens will sometimes try new and even magical cures on the strength of claims that regulators never saw or approved. The public does not invariably act in its own self-interest when faced with clever marketing. In the social sciences and especially the humanities it can be hard to discredit ideas that have a weak factual or logical basis, especially when the ideas are well presented and fit with existing preconceived ideas. Marketplace testing may work better for a field like economics, which is strongly data oriented, but even in economics genuine disproof can require years or decades and unproven ideas can persist for political or social reasons.

It is not clear that any reliable scholarly mechanism can ensure information integrity in the short term, despite contemporary publication requirements and editorial efforts. From a historical perspective, it is safe to say that the quality and reliability of information today is significantly better than it was hundreds of years ago, and a good information scientist should understand what an achievement that is, even if it is not an achievement for which the field can take any direct credit. Nonetheless, maintaining a public focus on the integrity of information should count as a contribution.

Conclusion

While the integrity of information represents a key focus of modern information science, scholars today should never imagine that contemporary circumstances and contemporary standards are any more eternal and persistent than those of the past. The artefacts of past authority systems remain, and one of the factors that seems particularly hard to escape is the advantage that well-funded organisations and well-to-do people have when making decisions about information reliability. Climate change offers a good example. The amount of reproducible scientific evidence about changes in the climate of the Earth faces no credible counter-evidence, but organisations and people with economic interest in the sources of climate change – such as major oil companies – discount their own role in the causes in order to continue their lucrative practices that make climate change worse. For the information scientist, this kind of context is an element in the integrity of information that cannot be ignored.

CHAPTER 9

Artificial Intelligence

What is artificial intelligence?

Artificial intelligence (AI) usually refers to systems that seem capable of solving problems in ways that resemble human thought. Christopher Manning (2020) provides one of the classic definitions: ‘Artificial Intelligence …, a term coined by emeritus Stanford Professor John McCarthy in 1955, was defined by him as “the science and engineering of making intelligent machines”.’

Like most descriptions, this one avoids defining what exactly ‘intelligence’ means. McCarthy’s definition differs from more recent versions that link AI specifically with computers, as if people never thought about making machines behave intelligently before computers. Information scientists know that most systems with the means to make variable responses to external stimuli exhibit some measure of intelligence. The idea of AI is old, even if the current name and hype suggest otherwise.

People have long looked for ways to outsource human thinking, and an important element of AI today is the ability for people to interact with that new intelligence. AI proponents typically define that interaction as a form of verbal exchange, because people associate words with thinking, but in fact people get information from a range of non-verbal sources and can interact with those sources without necessarily using words. One example is the automatic clutch, which responds to changes in road conditions (slope, for example) in ways that make human intervention unnecessary. It is not intelligence of the same kind as belongs to a large language model (LLM) capable of writing essays, but the response of the automatic clutch can sometimes exhibit more intelligence than a human driver. Other of the standard sources of non-verbal intelligence that humans use are visual and auditory. These will be discussed later in this chapter.

The idea that AI embodies a general and independent intelligence with human levels of creativity that works completely independently from human input is still largely science fiction, though that could change as systems evolve. Every system so far relies on human-provided information input, including the newest LLMs which depend on people selecting appropriate sources and writing appropriate code. Current AI systems depend also on human judgement about the value of the results. None of these AI systems think or operate as independently as Isaac Asimov’s robots. Science-fiction fears and expectations about AI have become so enmeshed in current discussions that they sometimes misrepresent what the current state of AI can do.

Artificial intelligence past and present

Identifying what the first forms of artificial intelligence were depends on the context in which people sought external help with making complex decisions. It is possible to argue that astrology was an early AI system, because many people believed that the movements of the planets told them how the world worked and how to respond to events. The scientific information behind astrological observations still holds up to a significant extent, though few today still take the stars seriously as a source of advice. The visible behaviours of planets and stars correspond largely to what ancient astronomers observed, but people today draw different conclusions from the movements and placements of the stars. In an information science sense the astrologers’ answers had an accurate technical basis, even though the validity of the astrological interpretation is at odds with modern science. In the past many people trusted the economic and political recommendations of their star charts more than some trust AI today.

Examining entrails represented another early AI system that the ancient world relied on. This goes back to ancient Etruscan, Greek and Roman times. ‘The fourth-century Athenian writer Xenophon describes an occasion when the mercenaries with whom he served were held up in hostile territory and short of food, and the entrails forbade them either to move on or even to go out foraging for supplies, for several days’ (Bowden, 2005). Reading entrails required picking the right animal and an interpretation that fit the expectations of the time. Divination was also practised in pre-modern China. The important fact is how widespread the trust was in these tools as a means for making decisions.

Other non-magical examples exist for how pre-modern people used tools for advice. The abacus was an early device for calculating. An abacus did not communicate in words, as modern AI systems do, but used a visual arrangement of beads in a two-dimensional array. The abacus is itself quite ancient, and versions were used in China, India and the Middle East as well as in Europe. According to Wikipedia (2025k), both South and Central American cultures appear to have had a similar calculation tool. For information science, one of the important features of AI systems is the ability to make reliable recommendations, even if only on specific topics. The reliability of the abacus has held up for millennia.

Many other simple tools provided reliable information, often using standard mathematics. The sextant is an example. Wikipedia (2025l) explains how a sextant works:

The estimation of this angle, the altitude, is known as sighting or shooting the object, or taking a sight. The angle, and the time when it was measured, can be used to calculate a position line on a nautical or aeronautical chart – for example, sighting the Sun at noon or Polaris at night (in the Northern Hemisphere) to estimate latitude (with sight reduction).

Isaac Newton may have been the first person to describe the way a sextant works (Liebman, 2019). By the 18th century the sextant had come into broad use for ships travelling long distances across the oceans, which meant that it was de facto an invaluable AI tool for both trade and military purposes.

In the mid-20th century car manufacturers began to use robots to make assembly lines more efficient. Mostly these were robots with flexible arms and the strength to handle parts that weighed more than a normal human being could manage. According to Wikipedia (2025m), the International Federation of Robotics estimated there were just under four million robots in 2022. Some were able to work without human control, and the earliest could react to external signals like light. These systems were consistent and replaced humans in tasks that were dangerous, repetitious and boring. Typically, a human supervisor would make sure that nothing went wrong. Factories were not the only place where semi-intelligent robots found work. Robotic cleaning systems for home use have been available for decades. Many can work around barriers, remember where they were in a room and recognise dirt. Their ability to interact with people is limited, but they can react to their environment when necessary.

Context awareness is a problem with most AI systems. Some investors use intelligent trading systems to buy or sell stocks based on an algorithm that embodies a trading strategy and sets trigger points for when buy or sell actions should take place. They analyse external data feeds using statistical tools to identify when to take action in the market. In general they do not interact with humans, unless someone stops the trading. The advantage of these systems is their speed in volatile market conditions. Their disadvantage is that their intelligence is limited to the set of instructions built into their algorithms, and, if circumstances change in unexpected ways, humans must intervene.

The context-awareness problem can also be seen in self-driving cars. In theory, self-driving cars could be safer because humans easily become distracted or make bad judgements, but the record of self-driving cars is far from perfect when conditions are abnormal:

California has ordered the company Cruise to immediately stop operations of its driverless cars in the state.… The move comes after one of Cruise’s driverless cars struck a pedestrian in downtown San Francisco earlier this month. The incident involved a woman who was first hit by a human driver and then thrown onto the road in front of a Cruise vehicle. The Cruise vehicle braked but then continued to roll over the pedestrian, pulling her forward, then coming to a final stop on top of her.

(Kerr, 2023)

Cummings (2023) warns about the degree to which software is error prone, especially in complex systems: ‘For example, if a self-driving car fleet operates in one city with one kind of bus, and then the fleet moves to another city with different bus types, the underlying model of bus detection will likely drift, which could lead to serious consequences.’ The point is that information scientists need to remember that contemporary AI systems are as context dependent as all other systems that were used historically to make decisions. One advantage that humans continue to have over AI-driven cars is an awareness of their surroundings, which is not as easily imitated as developers have hoped.

Early computing efforts at AI

Computer scientists have been thinking about how to turn computers into AI tools for as long as computers have been around. John McCarthy first worked on the computer language Lisp from the summer of 1956 to the summer of 1958, ‘when most of the key ideas were developed (some of which were implemented in the FORTRAN based FLPL)’ (McCarthy, 1979). One of the problems at the time was the limited size of computer memory. Prolog was a newer AI language ‘conceived by Alain Colmerauer at the University of Aix-Marseille, France, where the language was first implemented in 1973’ (Copeland, 2018). Both languages continue to be used today, but neither succeeded in enabling computers to understand the nuances of human language.

One of the common tests for whether a computer can imitate humans in conversation is typically called the Turing test because Alan Turing conceived it as an imitation game: ‘The interrogator is in a room separated from the other person and the machine. The object of the game is for the interrogator to determine which of the other two is the person, and which is the machine’ (Oppy and Dowe, 2021). Turing believed that by roughly the year 2000 (50 years from when he was writing) computers would be able to ‘play the imitation game so well that an average interrogator will not have more than 70 percent chance of making the right identification after five minutes of questioning’ (Oppy and Dowe, 2021). This has not happened, but has long represented one of the goals of AI. The degree of sophistication and complexity of the questions add another challenge for the Turing test. The simple fact is that no single AI system has yet convincingly passed the Turing test.

Expert systems were an early approach to create systems that could give reasonable advice for real-world operations. They were supposed to resemble AI systems with limited reasoning capabilities. Although expert systems were popular in the 1980s, their focus was too narrow.

What are ‘expert systems’? They are programs which reproduce human decisionmaking from a set of precoded rules. They are the offspring of artificial intelligence research, but unlike artificial intelligence, the intellectual scope of expert systems is narrow. They do not attempt to think, but only repeat what humans have thought. They capture expertise instead of creating it, and are in many respects like the conventional programs that working computer people deal with every day.

(Seadle, 1991, x)

The best expert systems could deal with some degree of uncertainty, but genuinely complex situations exceeded their capacity.

Programs could imitate certain kinds of intelligent human tasks. Among them were chess-playing programs, most of which failed when playing against top professionals,, until in 1996 when IBM’s Deep Blue defeated Garry Kasparov, the top-ranked player at the time in two out of six games. This was the best that a chess program had ever done, and inspired IBM to work on the system further until it defeated Kasparov in a new match a year later (Wikipedia, 2025n). A computer could calculate the consequences of moves far faster than any human, but the best chess masters do not rely only on combinational logic and on a sense of how positions could evolve. Bobby Fischer famously defeated his opponents by doing the unexpected. Chess computers continued to evolve, until Google programmers tried a neural network approach that resulted in 2017 in AlphaZero, which used neural networks that in effect let the system learn chess on its own. In that sense, it was a true AI, though one whose scope was limited to a board with 64 squares and figures that had closely prescribed movements.

Games offer many possibilities for AI-like research under constrained conditions. The popular US television quiz game ‘Jeopardy’ is an example. The game required players to answer factual questions in competition with others. The first to offer a correct answer won the points for that question. The challenges for an AI were not merely to understand the question (which it received in text form instead of orally), but also to search its databases quickly enough to decide whether it had a correct answer. Some human players would signal an answer before they were quite ready, just to be the first. Watson’s programming did not allow that, but over-fast responses did not always help the humans. Watson’s success made an impression, but, as with chess, Watson did not have to deal with complex context shifts. The rules and the kinds of questions remained the same throughout the game. Except for parsing the questions, it was less of a genuine AI challenge than a database search issue.

One of the goals of AI developers has been to create tools called ‘chatbots’ that offer online help independent of humans. In terms of corporate finances, a chatbot pays for itself when it can substitute for a far more expensive human. This does not mean that people using chatbots always feel that they have received a satisfactory answer. Companies may consider the chatbot a success any time a question does not get escalated to a human operator. Chatbots can often respond well to very specific factual questions. A study from October 2023 rated the answers of chatbots to medical questions:

In this cross-sectional study of 33 physicians across 17 specialties who generated 284 medical questions, chatbot generated predominantly accurate information in response to these diverse medical queries as judged by these academic physician specialists. The median accuracy score was 5.5 (between almost completely and completely correct), and the median completeness score was 3.0 (complete and comprehensive).

(Goodman et al., 2023)

The reliability rate varied a lot, though. Metz (2023) reports somewhat different results: ‘Now a new start-up called Vectara, founded by former Google employees, is trying to figure out how often chatbots veer from the truth. The company’s research estimates that even in situations designed to prevent it from happening, chatbots invent information at least 3 percent of the time – and as high as 27 percent.’

Chatbots increasingly rely on LLMs.

Large language models

LLMs grew out of work on natural language processing, which has long interested computer scientists. ‘In 1966, an MIT computer scientist, Joseph Weizenbaum, developed ELIZA, which is described as the first program using NLP [Natural Language Processing]’ (Foote, 2023). ELIZA was able to give responses to people that seemed so real that Weizenbaum’s secretary responded as if it were a real person. ‘[Weizenbaum] later added, “I had not realized … that extremely short exposures to a relatively simple computer program could induce powerful delusional thinking in quite normal people”’ (Foote, 2023).

This reaction is symptomatic of the first impression that people who are neither computer scientists nor information scientists still have today when encountering modern AI systems. It speaks well for the quality of the systems that they can create such an impression. It also means that information scientists have an obligation to make the limitations of LLM responses clear.

The large language algorithms are typically not transparent. The models consist of two core components: the language source and the analytical engine that puts the data (mainly words) together into plausible words and paragraphs. The models currently work only with text-based vocabulary, which cuts them off from non-verbal context information surrounding the words. That will likely change as the AI tools behind these systems grow more sophisticated, but that would also mean adding capabilities to collect visual and tonal and other forms of information. Text can describe the information that people get via their senses, but a strictly verbal description may leave out important social and emotional details. At this point computers can capture sounds and visual information effectively, but lack many of the nuances that matter for processing that information.

The choices about where an LLM learns words determine what AI engines can do. Ideally the sources match the purpose. Even when the whole of the World Wide Web is the source of words, not all models handle unknown languages. Linguistic discrimination is a known challenge.

LLMs can exhibit linguistic discrimination due to the uneven distribution of training data across languages.… We conduct this analysis with two datasets … The results show that LLMs exhibit stronger human alignment capabilities with queries in English, French, Russian, and Spanish … Moreover, for queries in English, Danish, Czech and Slovenian, LLMs tend to produce responses with a higher quality (with 0.1494 F1 score on average) compared to the other languages.

(Dong et al., 2024)

The four languages in the test described above belong to two Western European language groups and belong to countries that share considerable intellectual overlap. Since much of the academic writing today is in English because of the dominance of English-language journals, it is not clear how much impact the other languages have.

The sources for LLMs matter, because the AI is not just pulling words from a dictionary but is trying to fit the words to a particular context. The right combination of words matters: in many sentences an LLM could pick adjectives that would be grammatically correct but would give the phrase a different meaning. This is one of the areas in which an AI needs some degree of contextual understanding to be effective. That is one reason why large collections of academic articles are a popular choice, because they provide not only a scholarly vocabulary but also context information about what words and phrases belong together.

LLM systems typically go through a pre-training phase. As Mehra (2023) explains, the purpose is to ‘establish the fundamental language skills of LLMs’. In a sense, LLMs are like small children who must learn how to use words:

Most LLMs use general data, such as web pages, books, and conversations, as their pre-training corpus because it is abundant, diverse, and easily accessible. This helps improve their language modeling and generalization skills.… It is important to preprocess the collected text data to create a pre-training corpus for LLMs to remove noise, redundancy, irrelevance, and potentially harmful content.

(Mehra, 2023)

What to exclude from the pre-processing is as important as what to include. Removing duplicates matters too: ‘At the sentence level, low-quality sentences that contain repetitive phrases or words should be removed to avoid introducing repetitive patterns into the model’ (Mehra, 2023). Wikipedia, Google data and government data are commonly used as sources because they are large, diverse and tend to be free of copyright problems.

As with training children to speak, the training process for LLMs depends on teachers, in this case the models. ‘During training, the model is presented with a sequence of words and is trained to predict the next word in the sequence. The model adjusts its weights based on the difference between its prediction and the actual next word. This process is repeated millions of times until the model reaches a satisfactory level of performance’ (Dilmegani, 2024).

Size is a training issue: ‘By dividing the model into smaller parts, each part can be trained in parallel, resulting in a faster training process compared to training the entire model on a single GPU or processor’ (Dilmegani, 2024). From an information science perspective, this is another reason why it is important for people to understand how AI systems using LLMs work, because their reliability depends directly on their training. This is another reason why training information and the algorithms need to be made public.

The language models do not actually use words and letters in the way that humans do. The process is called ‘tokenisation’. ‘Large language model’ in Wikipedia (2025o) offers an explanation: ‘Because machine learning algorithms process numbers rather than text, the text must be converted to numbers. In the first step, a vocabulary is decided upon, then integer indexes are arbitrarily but uniquely assigned to each vocabulary entry, and finally, an embedding is associated to the integer index.’ The process of creating tokens varies from language to language. Wikipedia (2025o) claims that languages like German require 50% more tokens. Wiik (2024) explains: ‘This process has traditionally been tailored to each language based on its unique linguistic rules. For instance, an English tokenizer might handle contractions like “don’t” by recognizing them as single tokens, whereas a German tokenizer might split compound words to manage token size effectively.’

The tokenisation process adds context tags to indicate whether a word is a noun, verb or adjective. It also captures context elements by creating n-grams. ‘N-grams are sequences of “n” consecutive words, which provide a context that single words in isolation do not (e.g. “not good”) for capturing negations. They have been essential for building traditional language models that predict the probability of a word based on its preceding words’ (Wiik, 2024).

One additional linguistic transformation is to ‘stem’ the word, which means removing endings, and to ‘lemmatise’ a word by reducing the various forms to a single word. One additional feature of how LLMs use words is called ‘Byte-pair Encoding’. It ‘originally started as a data compression technique and was later adapted for use in natural language processing as a tokenization technique for subwords…. The algorithm starts with a vocabulary of individual characters and repeatedly merges the most frequent adjacent pairs of tokens into single tokens.… This approach helps manage a language model’s vocabulary size …’ (Wiik, 2024).

One of the common problems with LLM output is that they lose track of where particular sets of phrases came from, which is a reason why LLM-written essays can have plagiarism problems. How large this problem is remains an open question with very little reliable data, in part because most LLM-produced text have not undergone serious plagiarism checking. That is likely to change as more students and more researchers take advantage of the tools.

One solution is to build plagiarism control into the LLM output process. A disadvantage of that solution could be that detecting LLM-produced writing becomes harder. The broader ethical question is under what circumstances students and researchers should be allowed to submit work as their own intellectual product, when they relied on LLM to do the writing. It is too early to give a reliable answer to this question, because a great deal depends on our understanding of the degree to which authors can control LLM output. An important legal question has to do with copyright: ‘The prerequisite for copyright protection for AI supervision and AI-generated content is that human creativity in the materials used to train AI systems can be distinguished, so as to further promote the implementation of more complete legal supervision’ (Wu et al., 2024). This represents an area where information scientists can make useful contributions.

AI images and voice

AI systems can generate products other than text, and AI-generated images have become relatively common. There is nothing new about fake images. People and governments have been creating them for decades. The Soviet Union was especially known for doctoring photos when someone fell out of favour. What is new now is the ease with which AI can create convincingly authentic images, even without an original. The same process can be used to create fake audio and video. People create these ‘deep fakes’ for commercial or political purposes, or for blackmail and harassment. The following incident was described in a 2021 report by the US Department of Homeland Security about the risks of deep fakes:

Three teenagers, members of a cheerleading program in Bucks County, Pa., received a series of harassing text messages over the course of last summer. So did their parents and the owners of the gym where they practiced. Some of the anonymous messages contained doctored images and videos that attempted to incriminate the teenagers with fake depictions showing some of them nude, drinking alcohol or vaping. At times, the text messages took an even darker turn, telling at least one girl to die by suicide.

(Morales, 2021)

Information scientists should be aware of the degree to which deep fakes can be misused and abused.

The basic technology for creating these images resembles the way in which LLMs work: ‘An AI text-to-image generator often makes use of a machine learning technique called artificial neural networks that can receive input in the form of words, which it then processes to generate an image.… However, before they can be reliably deployed, neural networks require a lot of training’ (‘Alex’, 2024).

Images are broken down into tokens, much as is done with LLM. ‘Visual tokens are generated through ingenious techniques such as image segmentation, object detection, and feature extraction. By breaking down images into these meaningful visual elements, AI models gain the ability to identify objects, track their movements, and even understand the context of entire scenes’ (Kamali, 2023).

As with LLMs, the quality of the training process matters:

When you enter your words in an AI image generator, you’re feeding a computer algorithm (neural network) trained on thousands or millions of images from books, the internet, or other sources. This neural network then analyzes your request to understand the style, shape, color, and overall detail of what you want, relying on the dataset it was trained on to produce the final result. The higher the quality of the dataset, the better the quality of the results.

(Linto, 2024)

Unfortunately, the transparency of the training data is often limited, so that users do not know whose images went into the model. This is an ethical problem for sites offering to make deep fake nudes. Samelson (2024) writes:

At its core, the technology relies on algorithms that can detect patterns and features within a photograph, such as the outline of clothing and the human body’s form beneath it. These algorithms are trained on vast datasets of nude images to understand and replicate the appearance of the human body accurately … Using the learned patterns from its training data, the AI predicts what the person might look like without clothes and generates a new image by replacing the clothing with artificial nudity.

The easy availability of such tools (a simple web search finds them) means that anyone can create nude pictures that look genuine. In societies where nudity is socially unacceptable, deep fake nudes become a basis for blackmail and harassment.

From an information science perspective, deep fakes mean that an image may not be a reliable information source. The same problem applies to video, which AI tools can also fake. In broad technical terms, the same process that is used for words and images works for sound. An algorithm captures voices whose characteristics are stored as tokens, which makes it possible to assemble and reassemble them in different forms. This means that the combination of a person’s image, movements and voice can be reformatted in ways that the subject never intended or allowed. Controls on this kind of deep fake are currently as incomplete as copyright controls were earlier, but the consequences can be more embarrassing. Josh Butler (2024) reported in the Guardian about an instance in Australia: ‘Images of the finance minister, Katy Gallagher, and the foreign minister, Penny Wong, have been used in deepfake investment scam videos in the latest examples of a concerning trend of politicians being used in online fraud.’ The same problem can be seen in the USA. Zachary Wolf (2024) reported for CNN: ‘One distressing headline out of New Hampshire as voters prepared to cast in-person primary ballots was that a fake version of President Joe Biden’s voice had been used in automatically generated robocalls to discourage Democrats from taking part in the primary.’

Political abuse of the technology of deep fakes represents a dark side of AI.

Conclusion

For information scientists, AI can represent a valuable enhancement for analysing, retrieving and representing information when used in ethical and scholarly ways. The hype about AI capabilities will likely fade over time, as it has in the past, once people discovered that AI tools did not eliminate the need for human judgement. The job of the information scientist is not to debunk AI but to explain how to apply it reliably and ethically. In some ways, modern statistical packages represent an earlier form of AI, because they implement complex tasks faster and more reliably than humans, and even make some decisions based on the data. Their results are also more reliable than the calculations most humans do. Reliability is a technical goal to which many fields can and should contribute, but those working on AI technology should not forget the context issues that complicate solutions. Self-driving cars offer a good example. Truly reliable self-driving cars can and likely will save lives, but the AI controlling the car must have a level of environmental awareness that is better than the notoriously imperfect human attention span, whose lapses are common causes of car accidents.

The ethical issues involving AI are at least as complex as the technological issues, and many technicians working on AI have little philosophical training on ethical or legal issues. Deep fakes are just an example of some of the problems that the technology enables. From a programming viewpoint, there is no reason why those creating AI applications could not build controls into their systems to prevent abuses, and from a legal viewpoint there is no reason not to establish laws limiting some of the worst deep fake uses. These are issues where information scientists might reasonably paraphrase Isaac Asimov’s famous first law of robotics: an AI may not harm a human or, through inaction, allow humans to come to harm (Wikipedia, 2025p).

CHAPTER 10

Information Behaviour

What is information behaviour?

Marcia Bates (2010) defined information behaviour as

the currently preferred term used to describe the many ways in which human beings interact with information, in particular, the ways in which people seek and utilize information. Information behavior is also the term of art used in library and information science to refer to a sub-discipline that engages in a wide range of types of research conducted in order to understand the human relationship to information.

When discussing information behaviour, Greifeneder and Schlebbe (2022) also cite Tom Wilson’s (2000) definition: ‘the totality of human behaviour in relation to sources and channels of Information, including both active and passive information seeking, and information use’. These scholars are primarily describing a modern academic field. An information scientist could also consider the philosophical and historical implications.

The contemporary field of information behaviour focuses on how humans behave towards information. In the academic world information depends largely on language, and philosophers have long recognised that language is not a passive player. For example, Steiner (1987) cites Heidegger as saying: ‘Man acts as though he were the shaper and master of language, while in fact language remains the master of man.’ In plain terms this means that words as information carriers can have meanings beyond what the speaker or writer intends, since their connotations may express different meanings in different contexts, and because words from one language may not mean the same thing in another. A simple example is that the standard German word for a radiator (Heizkörper) does not imply something at all similar to a car radiator (Kühler). The former adds heat and the latter removes heat. Cross-language meanings challenge expectations.

How people behave towards information, and how information alters people’s behaviour, has changed over time, and is not just a matter of words. Symbols embody complex and variable information that has radically different meanings in different contexts. The Sanskrit word for swastika (सस्वस्तिक) means something like ‘conducive to well-being’ (Wikipedia, 2025q). The right-facing form of the symbol is as ancient as the Indus Valley civilisations, but its positive meaning changed in the 20th century when the Nazi party adopted it as their symbol and made it synonymous with antisemitism. The symbol did not change, but its 20th-century usage imparted a message that few can look at without remembering its Nazi connotation. There are many other symbols with strong meanings that in some sense speak to people. A simple cross may look like a letter ‘T’ in some contexts, but can also represent the Christian cross, which has strong religious connotations for church members.

When people encounter new information, their emotional reactions vary. Casual evidence for this is available in the daily newspapers. In terms of classic information seeking, it should be reasonable to think that people look for information they like, but the reality is that people encounter a broad range of information in their daily lives that cannot always be avoided.

Pre-modern information behaviour

Human behaviour towards information in the pre-modern world was different than it is today, in part because modern mechanisms for seeking or understanding information did not exist. Facts sometimes confronted people in ways that did not fit existing intellectual schemes. A comet, for example. Essentially, seeing a comet evoked thoughts that had no relationship to contemporary explanations about an object composed of ice and dust. Today people can find some form of factual information to explain almost everything. The pre-modern world had to struggle with unexpected mysteries, and people had to invent explanations to make sense of what they encountered in a context that they could understand.

As Greifeneder and Schlebbe (2022) write: ‘As part of information discovery, intentional discovery describes research that assumes that a conscious need for information exists, and that people pursue the information actively.… Accidental discovery, in contrast, is classified as the unstructured or unplanned discovery of information and refers to the accidental but useful finding of information.’

In the pre-modern world, accidental discovery was perhaps the norm. Greifeneder and Schlebbe also discuss information ‘non-use’ and ‘non-seeking’. Non-use and non-seeking played a significant role in a world where written records were relatively rare. Information was often purely oral and was easily forgotten, and most people lacked a clear awareness of the formal existence of information sources. People reacted to events, which was the form of information they knew best. The intellectual context meant that people interpreted the information in a different social context than people would today.

Insofar as records exist today about information seeking in the ancient world, that world was so culturally different that people’s reactions may seem illogical. Prayer was a common means for getting information, and some records exist about the prayers that people paid monasteries to repeat on their behalf. Some prayers took the form of information wishes, but the wording of the prayers tended to be formulaic. People might pray privately for foreknowledge about the weather, or pray for their crops, or for success in sporting events, or to know what their enemies were plotting – but much of what we think we know is speculation because private prayers were mostly not written down. The ancient military desperately wanted information to give them an advantage in battles, and they recognised the strategic value of information, but their sources tended to be imperfect, despite the best efforts of leaders and spies. Accurate maps represented a reliable information source, but historians know today that, despite the best efforts of commanders, Roman troops in places like England and Germany still lacked the detailed knowledge they needed about the terrain. In other words, the pre-modern world knew relatively little about information seeking and lacked key intellectual mechanisms to improve results.

When people do not understand the causes of events, they try to explain them in terms of their culture. This is a case where facts speak to people in ways that they so poorly comprehend that they have to translate them into something that makes sense. For example, ancient Roman society made no effort to address the Antonine plague of 165–180 CE, despite the huge number of deaths. They had no way of recognising the cause or (in modern terms) the real nature of a disease that was disrupting their society and killing up to one third of the population. They could only react to the plague in terms that made sense to them, and misconceptions about the consequences of the plague persisted for centuries. When William McNeill wrote his book on Plagues and Peoples (1976) a common assumption was still that Rome’s decline resulted from a form of internal (or moral) ‘decadence’, rather than as a consequence of depopulation due to the plague. Deaths from diseases like the bubonic plague in the 14th century represented another form of information that was practically invisible to people because they had no mechanism to understand the role of the fleas that carried the bacterium Yersinia pestis. The population’s behaviour when faced with the fact of the deaths led survivors to self-damaging behaviour such as self-flagellation to purge sins, and pogroms against Jews for causing the deaths (Britannica, 2025d).

Disease was not the only information that people misunderstood and misinterpreted. Today historians believe that the personal political interests of rulers led to wars in early modern times, but at the time the vital importance of religious issues seemed paramount. The Thirty Years’ War devastated the core of Europe, much as the fall of the Ming dynasty devastated China. One of the conventional causes of the Ming’s fall was the Mongol invasion that overthrew the Ming dynasty, but the fall may also have been a consequence of something less conventionally visible, because the Mongol invasion came at the time of ‘the deadliest phase of the Little Ice Age, when temperatures and rainfall plunged and world economies buckled’ (Brook, 2023). Recognising the information value of something like an ice age would have taken a level of careful record keeping, combined with the science to understand the consequences of climate change.

Anticipating the consequences of events was hard for people living in an era without systematic record keeping and reliable information sources in libraries or archives. They had little chance to address problems. Today people have better tools and more accurate information to deal with events, but even in modern times people have shown a remarkable ability to ignore factual information when it conflicts with their personal preferences or short-term economic and social interests. Climate change is only one example.

Role of language in information behaviour

Language is not the only means by which people create or communicate information. People need to remember that words often have an information value separate from their dictionary meaning and that language is more than just an information carrier. Steiner (1987) cites Heidegger as saying, ‘Language remains the master of man’. The connotations of words themselves provide forms of information that are often intransparent.
