Words can also change their meanings over time, sometimes in dramatic ways. A common example is the English word ‘beauty’. This concept has over the centuries implied quite different body forms in Western culture. The female body type that exemplified beauty in the 17th century can be seen in the paintings of Peter Paul Rubens: the women had a broad expanse of soft white flesh. Three centuries later the model Twiggy presented a very different standard of beauty that emphasised extreme leanness and thinness. The excess flesh of a Rubens nude would today imply overweight and no longer fits an ideal body type. The amount of visible fat on the women could also imply health consequences, even though it was considered healthy at a time when leanness implied poverty. The excessive thinness of the 20th century model Twiggy might have inspired modern young women to anorexia, which is itself dangerously unhealthy.

Even closely related languages like German and English have words that have different connotations and therefore carry different information, which can make translations challenging. An example comes from George Steiner’s (1987) discussion of Heidegger’s language: ‘“Innermost basic feature” is an honest attempt at rendering Grundzug. In German, and most notably in Heideggerian German, Grund portends intensely concrete but also numerous strains of rootedness, of earthly ancientness and provenance.’ The conventional dictionary meaning for the German word ‘Grund’ is ‘ground’ or ‘cause’ or ‘reason’, which leaves open many of the implications that Steiner is discussing. A more conventional variation in meaning can be found in the German word for ‘dinner’. A dinner is generally a large meal often but not invariably served in the evening. The German dictionary word for dinner is typically ‘Abendessen’, which explicitly means evening meal and generally implies a substantial one. A lighter evening meal is often called ‘Abendbrot’ (evening bread), which could correspond with the English word ‘supper’, which often suggests a light meal but does not imply having bread. The English word ‘dinner’ actually comes from the French term ‘dîner’.

Less closely related languages may have more divergent meanings and connotations. For example, in (simplified) Chinese the characters 火车 mean ‘train’, but someone who knows Chinese will realise that the first character of the two-character set means ‘fire’, which is a reminder of the origin of trains as coal-fired vehicles. The combinations of meanings in words often gives a clue to subconscious implications. The traditional version of the character 车 (車) gives a clue to its meaning: the four squares in the centre represent wheels for the classical Chinese chariots. Word etymology often gives a clue as to meaning. Even when the early meanings of words are largely forgotten, they can influence contemporary understanding. Examples of this abound. The game that Americans call ‘soccer’ is called football in most of the rest of the world, and the reason is historical. Cunningham (2023) explains: ‘Linguistically creative students at the University of Oxford in the 1880s distinguished between the sports of “rugger” (rugby football) and “assoccer” (association football).… By the 20th century, rugby football was more commonly called rugby, while association football had earned the right to be known as just plain football.’ Why the Americans and Canadians adopted the British term is apparently unknown. Football can literally just be any ball game played with the foot. Associations play a key role in both football (soccer) and rugby because it is the associations that determine the rules.

Words involving politics were especially subject to divergent meetings in the early 20th century. When the Soviets spoke about democracy, they meant a political system with elections controlled by the Communist Party, which many observers from Western countries called a dictatorship, even though voting rights were (technically at least) widespread. At this point, there is no special word for countries that allow large amounts of corporate spending to influence the results of voting. The cultural context in the West sees a meaningful difference between party influence and financial influence, and might even be shocked if someone equated the two. For Western Europe, democracy has often meant voting for members of a parliament, who in turn elect a prime minister, whose role is generally more important than that of the country’s president. The only major European country with a strong president is France.

At the beginning of the 20th century, democracy primarily meant voting rights only for men. It took decades of political pressure before women received the right to vote, and the expansion of voting rights changed at different times in different countries. Women first received the right to vote in the Soviet Union (1917), followed by Germany in 1918, the UK in 1918 (but only for women over 30, and not until a decade later for women over 21), the United States in 1920, France in 1944 and China in 1954 once the People’s Republic was firmly established. Insofar as the word democracy implies voting rights, its practical meaning depends on the local context and its traditions. Nonetheless, some historians and commentators act as if the word has a clear fixed meaning.

Information reliability

Information excess does not necessarily imply an overabundance of usable information, but an overabundance of distractions that are labelled as information and are sometimes even false. An important aspect of human information behaviour is learning how to distinguish unimportant or even wrong information from information that is genuinely (at least partly) useful. Ideally, people never seek flawed or dubious information, but it is often hard to avoid, perhaps especially in societies with limited information sources, where every form of news can seem interesting. Gossip may seem like a good source of local information, even though it is not always reliable. Proper information behaviour means learning how to distinguish between the fake and the real, especially when fake news seems more attractive.

In pre-modern times, excess information often came from gossip, both because there were few other sources and because people naturally took an interest in the people and events surrounding them. There was no way to check the reliability of local gossip, except to ask more neighbours, which could lead to rumours. Accusations of witchcraft in the form of gossip were common in Europe in medieval and early modern times, and could result in persecutions, trials, even burning women to death. Gossip could cause other forms of panic too, especially if times were bad and leading local figures believed the gossip. In a local context sometimes people with social ‘standing’ could serve as a reliability check, but measures of plausibility and reliability often had more to do with preserving order than with truth. In other words, mechanisms for testing the reliability of information relied less on facts than on people, except in cases where information that would count today as facts was too obvious to ignore.

Once newspapers became widely available, people had access to an external source of information that was not always reliable but was mostly better than neighbourhood gossip. On a modest scale, newspapers had been around in China since the Tang dynasty and in Germany and the Netherlands since the early 17th century (Britannica, 2025e). The Chinese version largely contained official news from the imperial court. The European newspapers were not official and often faced official censorship, which may have had a modestly positive effect in making publishers cautious. In later 19th-century Germany, publishers often named someone to be a ‘Sitzredaktur’, that is, an editor whose job it was to sit in prison when the authorities wanted to crack down on publications they disliked. The truth of a newspaper’s claims did not necessarily prevent the arrest, because many governments disliked reliable information that exposed their own failings.

The political opinions of so-called tabloid newspapers have tended to be right wing and nationalist, insofar as the tabloids offer any actual news with factual substance. Mostly they have focused on selling their papers to an audience that would not read complex stories with unfamiliar vocabulary, and have made their content as sensational as possible. Sexual innuendo was an especially popular way of attracting readers, as in this American example: ‘Sometimes the publishers wanted to inflame a situation such as during the weeks before the Spanish-American war of 1898. The famous Hearst newspaper drawing of male Spanish authorities strip searching a woman was successfully inflammatory, but was wrong in several respects: the woman was Cuban, not American, and the searchers were women, not men’ (Campbell, 2014, cited in Seadle, 2022, 33).

The best-known German tabloid, Bild Zeitung, was famous for headlines like that of 9 July 1984 on its 40th anniversary: ‘80 naked women locked up’ (Bild Zeitung, 1984). Vicki Barker (2015) reported on the US National Public Radio that ‘Britain’s most popular newspaper will reportedly stop featuring photos of topless women on its pages. The Sun began its “Page 3” pictures 44 years ago …’. The sensationalism represented a form of information excess that drove out more serious sources.

The same has happened since the advent of social media on the internet. The Pew Research Center’s ‘Social Media and News Fact Sheet’ (2023) stated: ‘Today, half of U.S. adults get news at least sometimes from social media.’ In the decades after World War 2, a few well-established newspapers and news broadcasters served as the main respectable sources for the educated middle class. A common criticism was that they represented the ‘establishment’ and deliberately excluded some kinds of information. This criticism was especially common among American students opposed to the war in Vietnam. Ben Kamisar (2024) reports that ‘Among registered voters, 54% described themselves as primarily traditional news consumers, while 40% described themselves as primarily digital media consumers.’ This split affects voting preferences.

It could be that news preferences determine election results. Polls involving people who rely on social media for news could affect the reliability of election predictions, even in a relatively traditional country like India. Guardian reporter Ellis-Petersen (2024) wrote: ‘According to a flurry of exit polls released on Saturday night, Modi and his Bharatiya Janata party (BJP) are looking at a decisive win and may even gain seats to win a two-thirds majority in parliament, which would allow the government to make far-reaching amendments to the constitution.’ In fact Modi did significantly less well than expected and did not get a majority in the Loc Saba (parliament). He had to rely on coalition partners to remain prime minister.

Some problems with information excess are traceable to unreliable popular sources. Fears about vaccines during the COVID-19 pandemic represent just one well-documented way in which unfounded reports cost lives, and many of those fears persist. Reports that discount climate change evidence offer another example. The problem is not just that multiple conflicting sources of information exist, but the very human inclination to disbelieve inconvenient news.

Information gaps

Information gaps represent the reverse of information excess. The gaps make people seek answers as best they can. Information seeking represents a standard topic in information behaviour and there is substantial research about it. Medical information seeking is typical and may account for as many as half of the searches on Google. However, the scholarly literature is not a source that the general public searches with much regularity, perhaps because the formal language of academic journals can seem opaque to ordinary readers. In other words, there are at least two forms of information gaps that information scientists need to consider: gaps for scholars, and those for the broader public.

The gaps for scholars cause fewer problems, because most scholars are accustomed to searching for information, and many electronic tools make the process easier, among them Google Scholar. There is no official measure of how large Google Scholar is, but a somewhat outdated estimate by Orduna-Malea et al. (2015) is ‘around 170–175 million unique records (regardless the number of versions of each record)’. Some estimates of the various scholarly databases put the numbers higher: ‘The results show that based on QHC [Query Hit Count], Google Scholar, WorldWideScience, and ProQuest … are by far the largest systems providing scholarly information, with each containing about 300 million records’ (Gusenbauer, 2019).

It is reasonable to assume that many of the entries in these databases are duplicates. This means from an information science perspective that the problem of scholarly information gaps begins to resemble the problem of information excess, with the important difference being that the information in these databases ought to be reliable.

The fact that articles appear in these respectable databases does not guarantee their reliability. The Retraction Watch blog reports regularly on problems in the academic journal literature. Anna Abalkina (2024) wrote in Retraction Watch, ‘I’ve cataloged 67 cases since 2013 of hijacked journals penetrating the [Scopus] database. I found 23 profiles of journals that contained links to a cloned version, and 33 cases of content from the cloned version of a journal that had not been peer reviewed appearing in the profile of the legitimate journal, while 11 did both.’ This represents only one example of the availability of hijacked journals in trusted databases, and the expectation is generally that such content may be untrustworthy. In other words, the hijacked journals may actually exacerbate the information gaps, since their review processes fail to catch errors or deliberate falsification. Instead of filling an information gap, they mask it.

Filling information gaps can be especially challenging for non-academics, because the range of tools and resources is more limited. Google and Bing provide answers to many common questions, but even their answers have flaws. One of the most common problems is not the fault of Google or Bing, but of the people who manage the websites they index. Searches for restaurant menus occur commonly, but not every restaurant keeps its online menus up to date, and the restaurant reviews that appear as part of these searches lack reliability. Anyone can fill out a reviewer form, but the sites make no attempt to ensure a random sample of reviews. This means that any customer with strong opinions could mislead readers. Professional restaurant reviewers mostly write for established journals or newspapers, but sites like TripAdvisor allow almost anyone to write a review. Tony Naylor (2013) wrote in the Guardian: ‘You can’t just look at the scores. And to read it in a way that is rewarding, you need to develop a set of personal idiot filters, far more powerful than anything TA could develop to police the site.’

Wikipedia can be a useful source for filling information gaps, but some caution is needed. Wikipedia does not use professional peer-reviewers, but lets any reader correct and update articles. In 2005 Jim Giles wrote in Nature that a comparison of Wikipedia and Britannica found that ‘Wikipedia comes close to Britannica in terms of the accuracy of its science entries’. Academic assessments are often more cautious, such as this from Harvard College Writing Program (2024):

There’s nothing more convenient than Wikipedia if you’re looking for some quick information, and when the stakes are low … Nevertheless, when you’re doing academic research, you should be extremely cautious about using Wikipedia. As its own disclaimer states, information on Wikipedia is contributed by anyone who wants to post material … If you do start with Wikipedia, you should make sure articles you read contain citations – and then go read the cited articles to check the accuracy of what you read on Wikipedia.

The Encyclopedia Britannica is another readily available general source of information that covers less than Wikipedia, but the Britannica editors often name the authors of articles and do enough fact-checking to protect their brand.

Fake information

Fake information has become a research area for information scientists because of its long-term damaging effects on scholarship and on rational political discourse. Identifying fake information requires a classic set of intellectual tools that have developed over the millennia and that the library community has long cherished as a means of guaranteeing the long-term quality of their collections. Fake information has a long history, especially in medicine, where claims about unproven cures have long found gullible consumers. Claims about cures generate non-trivial amounts of fake information, especially when they seem to have a small element of truth. Virtually every form of inoculation against diseases like smallpox or polio or COVID-19 carries a risk of negative side-effects. Such risks are not fake, but some people publicise the risks so loudly that they gain salience in some communities.

The first wide-scale use of inoculation against a specific disease was against smallpox, and it became available in the UK in the 19th century. The vaccination involved using pus from infected cows. Jess McHugh (2021) wrote:

Just as quickly as doctors heralded Edward Jenner’s revolutionary 1796 discovery that the deadly smallpox virus could be prevented with a cowpox vaccine, some Brits met the news with a superstitious distrust that bordered on hysteria. Opposition to vaccination would grow and evolve over the next 100 years to become one of the largest mass movements of 19th-century Britain. People refused the vaccine for medical, religious and even political reasons …

The opposition came also from within the medical establishment, and reflects some of the differences in the nature of medical training then and now:

In an 1805 pamphlet, William Rowley, a member of the Royal College of Physicians, warned against vaccination, threatening the direst possible side effects. Rowley (among others) went so far as to suggest that the injection of cow material into a human body could cause a person to begin to resemble a cow, sprouting actual horns out of his head and hoofs in place of feet.

(McHugh, 2021)

Medical research responded to the COVID pandemic with vaccines that provided protection against the worst effects of COVID-19, but a non-trivial portion of the American public expressed concerns, as Funk et al. (2023) reported: ‘A majority of those who have not been vaccinated say it makes them angry when COVID-19 vaccines are required (67% say this describes their views very well) and nearly as many (60%) say the idea that public health officials are not telling us everything they know about the vaccines describes their views very well.’ Political leadership can play a role: ‘Democrats are about twice as likely as Republicans to say the benefits of coronavirus vaccines outweigh the risks (84% vs. 40%).’ (Funk et al., 2023). Smith (2022) wrote about Donald Trump in the Guardian: ‘The ex-president’s customary applause turned to jeers when he encouraged supporters to get vaccinated and told O’Reilly that he received a booster himself.’

Information scientists can do little to combat fake information except to expose it with cogent and convincing facts, and to teach everyone how to distinguish fake from real information. The latter is especially hard because convincing people does not just involve academic logic but also the logic of readers, which invariably depends on their context. Context for fake information can involve education or scientific background, but more often involves what people’s friends and neighbours believe, what their sources of news are and their political leanings. Ivan Oransky’s Retraction Watch plays an important role in exposing fake information in the scholarly world, but it is just one voice – not quite a small voice in the wilderness, since many scholars (and information scientists) follow his posts, but not all publishers or universities take sufficient or appropriate action. Information scientists must always remember that economic interests and personal lifestyle can have a more decisive influence than science or pure logic.

Conclusion

If information behaviour is, as Marcia Bates wrote, the way that people interact with information, then the scope is enormous, and those interactions change constantly over time as new information and new needs arise. In the ancient world people trusted different information sources than scholars accept today. The trust mechanisms in their context relied on authorities for advice that lacked scientific reliability, which from a modern perspective meant that they had more fake information to deal with.

One of the key points in this chapter is that information itself is a player in how people behave towards concepts of information. The choice of language matters because words count as a chief information source and languages express a range of implicit meanings and connotations that can shift how people understand the information they read or hear. Oral communication is especially sensitive to the tone of voice and accent. A person with a lower-class accent may have less credibility with an academic audience, and more with a group that itself identifies with the way the person speaks. Songs can give words important subliminal information, as can images and smell. Smell is not one of the senses that humans rely on heavily for information, especially not in the modern world where electronic information sanitises any direct signals of smell or taste or feel, which must then be conveyed indirectly with words or images or not at all. Information scientists need to take all of this into account when considering people’s information behaviour.

CHAPTER 11

Data Science

What is data science?

IBM’s corporate website (IBM Corp., 2021) provides the following definition of data science: ‘Data science combines math and statistics, specialized programming, advanced analytics, artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning with specific subject matter expertise to uncover actionable insights hidden in an organization’s data. These insights can be used to guide decision making and strategic planning.’ This is a relatively neutral definition that reflects how many academic programmes understand data science, and the resemblance to computer science is not accidental. For US programmes especially the market implications for graduates play a role in why data science has become popular. Some see it as a soft form of computer science with lower maths requirements and no pressure to learn the more difficult computing languages. If a data science programme actually offered everything in the IBM definition, it could compete well with the average computer science programmes, but the reality is that many programmes offer much less serious technical training and rely on the marketing value of the name.

At present the term ‘data science’ appears in 112,000 entries in Google Scholar, but few attempt to define it clearly. One example is Nasution et al. (2023), who wrote a chapter called ‘What Is Data Science’ in a book on Data Science with Semantic Technologies. The authors begin by saying, ‘Without realizing it, data have been companions to humans throughout their existence’. Information scientists can readily agree, since data is a form of information. Understanding data ‘across various scientific fields – such as medicine and health, agriculture and production, engineering and industry, etc. – requires transformation into one general concept that is systematic and organized’ (Nasution et al., 2023). A key question is whether a single general concept can include all the nuances of the fields they list.

A more critical description of data science can be found in a 2023 article by Seadle and Havelka: ‘Data are the eunuchs of the information world because data and data sets are functionally sterile and meaningless without reference to the intellectual, spatial, temporal, and social environment that represents their context. A serious researcher can prove nothing with data without knowing where they came from and what they mean.’ The point of this description is that the strong focus on processing data leaves out essential questions about the meaning of data in the broader intellectual and social context. Anyone who has worked in a corporate computing environment at more than entry level should know that processing data is effective only when the purpose, the nature and the use of the data are clear. Useful data cannot be reduced to meaningless lumps of bits and bytes. Understanding the mechanics of how to process data matters only when the value of data is understood and the purpose is clear.

Of course the best data science programmes do not ignore the meaning and purpose of data, but those elements are core to information science. The University of California’s Berkeley School of Information (2024) has a well-regarded data science programme which describes itself as ‘A Rigorous, Holistic Curriculum: The multidisciplinary online data science master’s curriculum draws upon computer science, social sciences, statistics, management, and law. Students use the latest tools and analytical methods to work with data at scale, derive insights from complex and unstructured data, and solve real-world problems.’ It also includes ‘Information ethics and privacy’ and ‘Machine learning’ (Berkeley School of Information, 2024). The breadth of this programme may reflect the iSchools connection in addition to Berkeley’s long-standing intellectual tradition.

The Ludwig Maximilian University in Munich (LMU) has a programme that describes data science as:

the science of extracting knowledge and information from data and requires competencies in both statistical and computer-based data analysis. Students learn statistical and computational methods for collecting, managing, and analysing large and complex data sets and how to extract knowledge and information from these data sets. The program also comprises courses on data security, data confidentiality, and data ethics.

(LMU Munich, 2024)

A third example of a data science programme is the School of Information at Sheffield University (UK). Sheffield offers students ‘a set of fundamental principles that support ethical extraction of information and knowledge from data. Case studies will help to show the practical application of these principles to real life problems’ (Sheffield University, 2024). The description goes on to include ‘practical skills in handling structured and unstructured data, analysing and visualising data, data mining, as well as gaining hands-on experience of software tools used and their use in real-world settings’.

These are only three examples of the many international institutions that offer data science programmes. Common elements in these programmes are topics like statistics, ethics and privacy or confidentiality. What is striking is not only the overlap with computer science and statistics but the absence of topics not directly relevant to the job market. For many schools of information science, data science offers a way to re-envision their library science past in order to improve their job-market appeal by discarding much of the old humanist content.

A goal of this chapter is to describe where data science and information science converge, and what elements a data science programme ought to consider.

Data before 1800

Knowledge about units of information that would today be called ‘data’ certainly existed in pre-modern times, well before there were digital computers to process them. Some of the oldest Sumerian records were accounting records, and the information recorded in the clay tablets was a form of financial data by any modern definition. Ashley Huang (2017) describes a Sumerian abacus that ‘had a clay board that was scored with columns. The columns were numbered from right to left in increasing magnitudes of 10s or 60s. They had shaped clay tokens that were assigned numeric values, in units of 1s, 10s, 60s, 600s, and 3600s. To represent a number, they place the tokens in the board columns (Wikipedia, 2025k).’ This abacus has a different appearance than the Chinese suanpan (算盘) from the Han dynasty, and also from the modern abacus, but intellectually the principle was essentially identical (Norman, 2004). Neither Sumaria nor Han China used a decimal system, but results from calculations were comprehensible and reliable, which made them ideal numerical data-processing tools.

Financial records were not the only form of data that needed processing in the pre-modern world. Almost every person and physical object counted as data that needed to be allocated into categories to become useful. For example, the military needed information about how many soldiers were available in what location at what time. Their training mattered, as well as the arms that were available and how well the soldiers could use them. Information about transportation was equally important in a world where getting from one place to another involved keeping track of shoes, horses, ox carts, and boats or ships for longer journeys. In the modern world it is easy to discount the importance not only of knowing these data points but of allocating them to categories that made sense, even though precision was hard. In the time of Julius Caesar the Roman army structured its military in units called ‘centuries’ because the centuries were supposed to have 100 men with a ‘centurion’ to command them. In fact the units rarely stayed at full strength. However, the weapons were standardised. They included a shield, a short sword and a spear, and no army could remain effective without some reserve of these standard-issue items.

An important form of data in earlier eras was the status of people. For example, the Roman and Greek societies included slaves, who had obligations towards their owners but also had more rights than did American slaves in later centuries, and keeping track of those rights mattered. Knowing the skill sets of a slave mattered too, since they were not used only for hard labour in the fields. In the ancient world they might be teachers or skilled in a particular craft. Records about a person’s religion represented another form of information that mattered in a society with a range of greater and lesser gods and cults. Roman emperors before Constantine regarded Christianity as potentially dangerous, and the government periodically attempted to eliminate them, which for the Christians increased the importance of keeping track (often secretly) of who their members were and where they were.

In the ancient and medieval worlds, the only way to handle most forms of non-numerical data was to record it in appropriate categories. Often this organisation occurred orally by repeating facts about people, places and things. Poetry was a good memory tool. The degree to which memory worked varied widely. Literate people kept written records, but there is no clear agreement on the level of literacy in the Roman Empire, and likely it varied over time. Generous estimates suggest 15%, but without a clear scholarly basis. Leonard Curchin (1995), writing about Spain, suggests at most 5–10%, which may be low for urban centres like Rome. From an information science viewpoint this means simply that data science, insofar as it could exist at the time, had limited capacity for analysis.

Smaller communities could sometimes cope better with data just because fewer details were involved. Specialisation helped, of course. Those responsible for a particular landholding needed only to remember the traditional implements and people and crops. Military commanders needed only to remember how their units worked. Since major changes were rare, memory functioned moderately well. The farming technology tended to remain the same in particular locations in most societies, in part because no one invented new tools or planted new crops as long as the old ones served the purpose and markets remained stable. That is not to suggest that no change occurred. Weather problems, invasions, illnesses all disrupted the mechanisms for categorising the data of the time. The market changes occurred mostly because of external circumstances, not because entrepreneurs had ideas. Entrepreneurs mostly did not exist. This meant that the aspects of modern data science which are focused on analysing change had little relevance.

The situation changed in the medieval world, not because new forms of processing data came into being but because growing literacy and the invention of printing increased the number of people who could handle more and more complex data. The situation in China was not fundamentally different, though literacy probably remained very limited until after the Communist revolution. A UNESCO report by Tiedao Zhang (2005) suggests that ‘[t]he fifth national census in 2000 shows that, the illiterate population of above 15 years of age has decreased to 86 million, that is, a 100 million reduction of illiterate population during the last 10 years …’. Printing was of course well established in China by European medieval times, which meant that Chinese institutions had the means for recording data.

Data 1800–1950

From an information science viewpoint, the situation began to change in the 17th century, due to factors such as a substantial increase in international trade. Trade meant more information about a range of unfamiliar foods, objects and cultures, and that meant that the ‘data science’ of the time needed new categories. Contact with India meant, for example, new religions, new spices, and new ways of thinking mapped imperfectly to existing ways of organising knowledge.

Unfamiliar languages counted as a new form of data, and linguistic scholars worked hard on understanding their roots and linkages. Fellmann (1978) writes: ‘It is customary in the linguistic literature to date the first scientific Sanskrit grammar written by a European to the Grammar of the Sankrita Language of Sir Charles Wilkens, published in London in 1808 …’ Fellmann (1978) also notes that in fact the earliest Sanskrit grammar ‘may be dated ca. 1660, being composed by the German missionary Heinrich Roth, a native of Augsburg … His work, it is true, was never published, and thus had no effect in Europe …’

The main spoken language in northern India is called Hindustani and includes both modern Hindi (written in the Devanagari script) and Urdu (written in the Persian script). Anna Pytlowany (2018) wrote: ‘To any Indologist, Instructie off Onderwijsinge der Hindoustanse en Persiaanse talen (1698) is instantly recognizable as the iconic “first grammar of Hindustani” – just as the name of its author, Joan Josua Ketelaar, is familiar to scholars of Dutch colonial history.’ Recognition for the southern Indian languages came slightly later. Krishnamurti (2024) wrote: ‘In 1816, Englishman Francis Whyte Ellis of the Indian Civil Service (at the time a division of the East India Company) introduced the notion of a Dravidian family.’

Merchants in the 18th century understood the importance of language for their business, not just to sell their products but to understand the meaning of new foods or objects or tools or anything quantifiable. Data ideally included metadata to explain meaning and context, which was no less important than it is today. Before 1950 language was the chief analytical tool to let people analyse and categorise valuable discoveries. A simple example was the American fruit called tomato and the vegetable called potato, both of which grew to have culinary value in Europe. When travellers encountered them, they had to learn from the natives what they were. It took decades before their popularity reached a point where counting them had commercial value. According to Giulia Bortolotti (2023), ‘It wasn’t until 1694 that the first recipe using tomatoes was published in the Bel Paese – courtesy of Neapolitan chef Antonio Latini and his book “Lo Scalco alla Moderna” (“The Modern Steward”). It was not until the nineteenth century that the tomato found its way across Italian kitchens beyond Naples.’ The potato arrived in Europe with the Spanish explorers, but, as a potentially poisonous nightshade, the plant did not gain acceptance until the 17th century. It became important as a food source once the population exploded and potatoes were needed as a cheap source of nutrition (Wikipedia, 2025r).

The Industrial Revolution occurred at quite different times in different countries. Generally, historians talk about it starting in the UK, which is true for certain aspects like coal mining and iron smelting, and people often associate trains with the Industrial Revolution, but Iowa State University (2016) claims that ‘Railways existed as early as 1550, in Germany. These pathways of wooden rails called “wagonways” were the beginning of modern rail transport, making it easier for horse-drawn wagons or carts to move along dirt roads.’ Data about maintenance needs was critical. In the early 19th century data about railway tracks, the condition of the engines, the number of passengers mattered, and could only be managed using ink and paper tools, plus a good understanding of what information mattered. Ticket sales in particular required a level of social organisation to take in money, give out paper tickets and to make sure that only legitimate passengers boarded the trains.

Today data scientists might care about the algorithms for calculating ticket prices, for which there was no precedent except by analogy with travel by horse-powered carriages. The price was within the reach of many citizens, including those willing to ride in discomfort on the carriage roof. Speed was a major attraction of the railway in its earliest days. Increasingly, people also valued the distance they could travel in a comparatively short time. The data for calculating what a middle-class traveller might pay was not available in any modern sense. The railways substituted a broad range of information including expectations, needs and the subtle attraction of modern technology, all of which an information scientist understands as part of the context of the time.

Data in context

As noted earlier, one of the key differences between information science and data science is an awareness of context as a decisive factor. Descriptions of data science focus relentlessly on modern applications. While the context for data science is largely contemporary business and operational needs, this will likely change over time, as long as data science graduates from top schools remember the librarian’s and the information scientist’s awareness of the importance of contextual breadth. A focus on the present is typical of technical fields trying to make an impact, and it is only after they mature that they remember the past. The path is not always straightforward. A century ago, economics had a strong historical sense, which economists later rejected in favour of a belief in timeless mathematical models. Once the algorithms ceased to appear to solve all problems, the interest in history revived. It is hard to reduce something as complex as human behaviour to an algorithm. Change over time is typical. When computer science was new, its training emphasised pragmatic technical aspects such as writing programs. It was not unusual for a doctoral project to deal with compilers. Today, sophisticated computer science dissertations discuss behavioural as well as technical issues. Doubtless, data science will change too.

One of the most data-centric professions is actuarial science, and those who want to become actuaries need proficiency in mathematics because risk analysis depends heavily on probability calculations. For decades, computers have done most of the actual calculating. An actuary must understand the math, but must also understand the nature of the risks. Life insurance is a typical example. It matters whether the person or persons in question are male, because the risk factors are different for men than for women. It is also important whether they smoke, or are overweight, or have a history of high blood pressure. Understanding the meaning of the data matters more than the simple processing of the data points. The best universities do not generally offer programmes explicitly called actuarial science. Future actuaries at top universities generally get degrees in mathematics or perhaps in business. One of the future risks for data science could be that it becomes so narrowly focused on the processing aspects that top universities lose interest.

Data science programmes often mention topics on data ethics and confidentiality, and the legal issues surrounding data have received more attention in recent years as the EU’s General Data Protection Regulations (GDPR) have come into legal force in most EU countries, which means that companies wanting to do business in the EU must comply. Karthik Kashyap (2022) reported in the Spiceworks blog that a ‘study found that 94% of [US] companies that must comply with GDPR were partially or [completely] non-complian[t] with the requirements’. Here is an example: ‘Ireland’s Data Protection Commission (DPC) yesterday announced the conclusion of an ongoing investigation involving WhatsApp regarding the violation of GDPR laws. Accordingly, DPC slapped the instant messaging service provider with a record €225 million fine’ (Kashyap, 2021). It is certainly true that many people do not understand GDPR compliance, partly because the regulations are complex, but perhaps also because the US corporate world shows little interest in non-US regulation. An interesting question will be to what degree US data science programmes cover GDPR and other foreign laws.

China also has data protection laws. Arendse Huld (2023) wrote: ‘2023 saw the implementation of several new regulations on data protection and cybersecurity in China, with a particular focus on cross-border data transfer and personal information protection. However, in an effort to improve the business environment, China’s cybersecurity authorities are also considering easing some requirements on multinationals and foreign companies.’

Different legal frameworks add complications to teaching students about data ethics and confidentiality, because it is not always clear what the laws may or may not allow, especially for those who cannot read the laws in the original language. Context issues force themselves on data science programmes more and more, which may well mean in the end that these programmes resemble information science with a name that seems more marketable in some locations.

Types of data

The argument of this section is that data are too complex to pretend to have any inherent uniformity. Data science, no less than information science, always needs to deal with a range of information types and formats. If all data were simple units representing something concrete, managing data would be relatively straightforward, but even data durability represents a complex topic. Simple examples abound: canned foods are not unchanging things, and now always have expiration dates. Clothes go out of fashion, and computers age at variable speeds, depending partly on the manufacturer, but their useful lifetime rarely exceeds a decade. Financial data are even less immune to change. The amount of money in a bank account may seem to represent something stable, but inflation can erode its value, as can bank charges. Interest payments may increase, which is also a value change. The point is that managing data of almost every sort means looking beyond the data themselves. Any good data science programme, like every good information science programme, must take these complicating factors into account.

Perhaps the simplest types of data include counts of what is happening at any one time. Countries with speed limits often have devices that track the speed of vehicles in a particular location at a particular time. The number of cars violating the speed limit represents concrete data points, which data scientists could compare to similar numbers at similar locations, assuming the measurements were all done using the exact same numerical speed limit rather than ones with a grace period calibrated to accept variations. Even then, true standardisation is hard, given cultural and other variables: a tolerance for speeding is greater in some countries and in some US states than in others. That does not make the data wrong, but it can and probably should influence any analysis.

Voter surveys are an area where counting may seem standardised, but answers to surveys vary depending on the reliability of the sample and on how honestly respondents answer. David Dutwin (2024), writing in Scientific American, explains that one problem with election polls is that people change their minds. Further:

Second, polls have real margins of error, which are reported too modestly. True ranges of error in election polls average around 5 percent, and many elections are won and lost with much smaller margins. Third, election polls have a unique characteristic not encountered in any other survey: they survey a population that does not (yet) exist. Election pollsters must predict who will actually vote. Their likely-voter models tend to be 80 percent accurate, leaving quite a bit of imprecision in deciding who among those polled should actually count in a ‘horse race’ estimate.

(Dutwin 2024)

Predicting outcomes represents an attractive goal for any form of scholarship, but the most reliable analysts are cautious. Elliott Morris (2024) wrote on FiveThirtyEight that ‘538 uses polling, economic and demographic data to explore likely election outcomes’. He adds the caveat: ‘It’s not meant to “call” a winner, but rather to give you a sense of how likely each candidate is to win.’

In theory, data from natural sciences like physics ought to generally be unambiguous, which is how scientists understood ‘atoms’ at one time, but even the true nature of an atom has become complicated since the early 20th century, when Niels Bohr proposed a new model for the structure of an atom that included electrons, protons and neutrons. The model became more complex over time as Max Planck developed the concept known today as Planck’s Constant and Albert Einstein described the nature of special and general relativity. Further research into the nature of elementary particles continues to refine how scientists understand their interactions. Even at the most atomic level, data are rarely just data, though there are occasions when it makes sense to treat them as if they were, in order to discover something new.

Biological data are even more varied than physics data. Even one-celled organisms have considerable variety and structure and can have very different effects on human bodies, depending on their genetic make-up. The complexity of living data means most forms of processing such data need to take factors into account that could affect the meaning of the result. Cattle ranchers, for example, need data about the herd they are bringing to the market, and that data needs to include not only the number but the types of cattle, their health and, ideally, their weight to be sure that the dealers who buy them do not cheat.

For human medicine the data issues are more involved, and oversimplification can be dangerous. When the World Health Organization (WHO) collects data, it must rely on its members. WHO (2024) writes that ‘A core component of WHO’s support to Member States is to strengthen their capacity to collect, compile, manage, analyze and use health data mainly derived from population-based sources (household surveys, civil registration systems of vital events) and institution-based sources (administrative and operational activities of institutions, such as health facilities).’

Unfortunately, the quality criteria for these data are not always transparent, and agencies must make do with what they can get. Collecting health data even in a developed country can involve politics. An example comes from the COVID-19 pandemic, when then US President Donald Trump ‘encouraged his top health officials to study the injection of bleach into the human body as a means of fighting Covid’ (McGraw and Stein, 2021). His suggestion appears to have had no basis in fact, but may well have sent researchers on a quest for worthless data. The US National Institute on Aging (2023) put together a set of criteria when checking sites. What they do not say is how to know whether the data in the recommendations are trustworthy, only that they should come from experts.

Public and private data

Rules about data privacy are both legal and cultural. Some extremes are clear: access to data about state secrets, especially military secrets, are generally restricted. Julian Assange ‘spent nearly seven years in the Ecuadorian embassy in London in an effort to avoid prosecution and an additional five years in British prison fighting extradition to the United States’ (Ray, 2023). The data that Assange exposed had a level of protection that showed it was clearly not for public consumption: ‘Numerous independent cybersecurity experts and U.S. law enforcement agencies confirmed that the data had been obtained by hackers associated with Russian intelligence agencies’ (Ray, 2023).

Most data exposure cases do not result in imprisonment, but some end up in law suits. A British website run by the registered charity Protect writes on its blog: ‘Under the DPA [Data Protection Act], there are a number of civil and criminal offences relating to the breach of personal data. However, the broadest, most serious, and most likely to apply is that of “unlawfully obtaining personal data” (section 170(1))’ (Protect, 2024). The rules vary from country to country, but many corporations are ready to take legal action against people exposing their data.

The risks for exposing personal information depend on the kind of information and the circumstances. Most universities today have rules about what questions their researchers can ask in a survey, and what steps they must take to protect any data that subjects give them. The University of Sheffield, for example, has a policy that ‘covers any staff or students who may be involved in research or other activity that requires them to process or have access to personal data (see section 2.2), for instance as part of a research project or as part of professional practice activities’ (Thompson, 2024). The policy goes on to list exactly what is included: ‘Personal data is any information relating to a living individual who can be directly or indirectly identified, by reference to an identifier, such as a name, an identification number, location data, or an on-line identifier or to one or more factors specific to the physical, physiological, genetic, mental, economic, cultural or social identity of that person’ (Thompson, 2024). This means that scholars doing social research must know how to make the data they collect as anonymous as possible, and must have ways of keeping it secure. Many universities now have secure sites where researchers may (and ought to) store any form of confidential information.

Some data is meant to be public. Health statistics provide an example, and most countries make the information available at a high level of granularity. These data matter for people who are making travel plans or taking jobs in an unfamiliar location. Physicians often use public health statistics to recommend what inoculations a patient should have when travelling. They must also consider environmental data for patients who have conditions like asthma. Data for travellers can also mean information about public safety, and the differences between purse snatching, armed robbery and rape or murder. One of the problems with public data is that the different levels of aggregation matter, depending on the degree to which aggregation is in fact concealing problems by grouping them. In the US, state legislatures use census data about race and ethnic background to influence where to draw congressional boundaries. The US census data is public, and the details about race, age and location feed into efforts that fit the negative term ‘gerrymandering’, which is illegal in some locations. One of the reasons for protecting private data is how easily the data can be abused, even in the aggregate.

Conclusion

Data science is not the same as information science, although the two have much in common. Data science today defines itself as a technology field. One of the reasons for this may be that data science is trying to make itself more attractive for corporate recruiters, who want people with strong technical backgrounds. Some corporate recruiters seem to regard information science as being too closely related to the humanist side of library science to fit the needs of their organisations. Data science is a new field that is likely to develop differently in different contexts and different countries as the interest in it grows.

In the 1990s, when universities began to recognise information science as an official academic field, it seemed like an attractive technical specialisation for many students in library schools. At that time the term ‘information processing’ referred broadly to computing activities, and many of the new information science programmes contained strong technical aspects, such as teaching programming languages to their students. In that sense, information science had a strong resemblance to data science today. It seems reasonable to imagine that the two fields will grow to become indistinguishable in time, regardless of which name takes priority.

CHAPTER 12

Non-verbal Information

What is non-verbal information?

Non-verbal information includes all sources that do not primarily rely on the use of words. One of the advantages of a face-to-face meeting, as opposed to written messages, is that there is more nuanced information in human expressions and in the tone of voice than most people are capable of putting in words. This form of non-verbal information may not count as data in a conventional sense, but it is certainly information that can influence how people understand the meaning of an oral message. The non-verbal information is quintessentially context based, and has strong cultural aspects that do not appear in written form. A facial expression such as a smile or a frown can indicate whether a person takes the information seriously or positively and whether or not the speaker trusts the information. Most people have relatively poor control over their facial expressions, unless they are trained actors. This means that impressions that the speakers might not intend to convey can also be read into their words. Something as simple as a raised eyebrow can make clear how a listener is reacting to a verbal message. A person chairing a meeting, or a teacher leading a class, is generally able to see the listeners’ faces and to judge their reactions by reading their expressions.

Tone of voice is another important factor in communicating information. A person who speaks forcefully and clearly gives an impression of control that matters both in politics and in the classroom. The quality of pronunciation also gives a clue about background, and especially about class background for communities where such assumptions matter. Grammar may matter less, but it serves as another indication of educational background and bad grammar can undermine credibility. The degree to which grammar and accent matter varies enormously with the situation. The ability to project one’s voice was a factor in public speaking, especially before microphones became ubiquitously available. Even speaking into a microphone requires training, because of the way microphones are tuned. Speaking too loudly into a microphone gives the impression of yelling.

Body language is another indicator of attitudes. Crossed arms may indicate defiance. A person holding both hands up may indicate surrender. A head tilted to one side can indicate doubt. Hand gestures are particularly important: the middle finger raised is the equivalent of an obscenity. ‘As in Western cultures, giving someone your middle finger is offensive in Chinese culture, signifying disrespect’ (Chairman’s BAO, 2024). A hand that is clutching the thumb means wishing someone good luck in German, much as touching wood or knocking on wood represents the same wish in British or American English. In China, wrapping a hand around a fist can signal a good luck wish as well. A handshake is generally seen around the world as a friendly sign. In some cultures it is appropriate for a man to offer a hand to the woman first, and in other cultures that is inappropriate unless the woman herself makes the offer. In Chinese culture, it is typical to let the highest-ranked person go through a door first. In many Western cultures, a polite man always lets the woman go first.

This chapter will look beyond human expressions and practices to discuss the kind of information that images and music embody, as well as the information that smell conveys, even though it is generally the least discussed of the human senses in academic writing.

Pre-modern non-verbal information

Since animals have no formal vocabulary, they must rely entirely on non-verbal channels for information, and the situation was probably similar for early humanoid species. The discovery of language made sharing sensory information easier insofar as the vocabulary sufficed. We know that cultures develop words for things that matter to them. Snow serves as a good example. There is a widespread claim that Eskimos have up to 100 words for snow, because the different types of snow matter to them.

Even if we exclude the sorts of terms that some have suggested should not count in our tally of snow terms, it still appears that Inupiaq at least has an extensive vocabulary for snow and ice. It would surely be a surprise if Inuit people did not pay special attention to snow and ice, which are important features of the landscape throughout most of the year.

(Kaplan, 2003)

The role of language serves here to confirm the importance of the tactile aspects of snow. It is only through vocabulary that scholars today gain insight into the non-verbal information that has played a role in people’s lives.

Sounds certainly played an important role in the lives of early hominids, and play an equally important role once modern humans begin to explore new territories and encounter new phenomena. The roar of a lion can serve as a warning. For hunters, the sound of sheep might indicate where the next meal might come from. The sound of water could have been important to early travellers who had limited means for carrying liquids. Anyone who tends animals will recognise that some sounds express fear, or hunger, or even pleasure. Interpreting these sounds is part of caring for the animal. Some animals communicate with humans using sound in ways that are more effective than others. Cats do not have a vocabulary per se, but purring conveys positive emotion. The domestication of animals may indicate one of the important ways in which people use non-verbal information to understand their environment. Animals communicate without words.

Smell is often an undervalued sense, and yet for early humans it was important because it allowed them to make judgements about the quality and freshness of their food. Rancid meat has a distinct warning smell, and the scent of a flower like belladona can indicate whether it is poisonous. Scent also indicates whether a piece of fruit is ripe enough to eat. Modern vocabulary does a poor job of distinguishing various scents. Yet there are businesses such as restaurants where a good sense of smell is central for the person cooking. Distinct odours from exotic cuisines can give a clear indication of their cultural origin. There is plenty of information available via smell, but most humans either classify it vaguely or ignore it. Some Western organisations make use of certain smells for ceremonial purposes. Incense in a religious service is one clear example. Smell is also a sign that something is no longer fresh and clean. In early modern times people perfumed themselves to hide body odours that modern deodorants disguise. People have often used scents such as sandalwood, or lavender, or jasmine to give the sense that a room is fresh and clean.

In the medieval world, bells had an important social significance that lingers to this day. Arnold and Goodson (2012) wrote: ‘By Innocent III’s time, bells had long been established at the core of Christian observance. Indeed some historians have thought that the ecclesiastical use of bells stretches back to late antiquity, and various writers have noted the conjunction of bells, liturgy, and community.’ The role of bells in telling the time lasted well into the 20th century. For example, alarm clocks use bells to indicate when a person should wake up. In many countries, a bell is used to indicate when classes start in a school, or when there is a recess, or lunchtime, or when it is time to go home. Other uses include doorbells that indicate the arrival of a visitor. Bells also indicate when food is ready to come out of an oven or microwave.

Many other non-verbal sounds play a role in modern life. Honking a car horn in a traffic jam indicates impatience. Distinctive noise makers are often used as part of a New Year’s celebration. The sound of a smart phone vibrating is not just a tactile signal, but also an aural one that lets people know someone is trying to reach them. In the American Midwest, people treat the first chirping of a robin as a reliable indication that spring is starting. The importance of hearing sounds can perhaps best be understood through the role that being able to hear appears to have in dementia. A Johns Hopkins University (2022) website reports that ‘In a study that tracked 639 adults for nearly 12 years, Johns Hopkins expert Frank Lin, M.D., Ph.D., and his colleagues found that mild hearing loss doubled dementia risk. Moderate loss tripled risk, and people with a severe hearing impairment were five times more likely to develop dementia.’ This represents one of many indications of how important sounds are as a source of human information.

Information in paintings

Humans, like most land-based animals, depend heavily on visual information about their surroundings, which is one reason why paintings matter to scholars. Much of what we know today about the life of the ancient Cro-Magnon people comes from the paintings they left in caves in France. The scenes depicted animals that the Cro-Magnon people knew or hunted, and they were not the only early painters. Oktaviana et al. (2024) found Indonesian cave paintings that may be as much as 51,200 years old and depict ‘human-like figures interacting with a pig, [which] is now the earliest known surviving example of representational art, and visual storytelling, in the world’.

Such paintings effectively transmitted information well before writing existed. Egyptian pyramids have paintings that survived because the buildings protected them from the weather. China has a long tradition of landscape and other painting that goes back at least to the Zhou and Han dynasties (Wikipedia, 2025s). The wall paintings that the volcanic ash preserved in Pompeii give a vivid picture of Roman life in 79 CE. It was typical that these paintings told stories, such as in a surviving double fresco where ‘the god Apollo is seen trying to seduce the priestess Cassandra. Her rejection of him, according to legend, resulted in her prophecies being ignored. The tragic consequence is told in the second painting, in which Prince Paris meets the beautiful Helen – a union Cassandra knows will doom them all in the resulting Trojan War’ (Amos et al., 2024).

Paintings played a similar narrative role in the medieval world, especially for those who could not read or write. They often told stories about lives of the saints for whom local churches were named. Paintings played a similar narrative role for Buddhism, both in India and in China. The purpose of painting was often to give concrete shape to figures in stories. Perhaps the most common stories in the Christian world were the birth and crucifixion of Jesus, which painters reproduced again and again. Icons played a significant role in Byzantium, which inherited elements of style from ancient Greek painting. As a general rule, medieval paintings did not produce plausibly accurate human portraits until approximately the 15th century. In the 16th century painters like Pieter Bruegel the Elder began to paint peasant festivals and everyday scenes that continue to serve as valuable sources of information about the social life of the time. Lucas Cranach the Elder produced exceptionally lifelike portraits in roughly the same era. Rembrandt followed in the same tradition with images that continue to be valued in the modern world for their realism. Rembrandt also painted scenes out of the Bible, but with a different tone than the medieval iconography. The visual horror of Rembrandt’s painting of the blinding of Samson still upsets some modern viewers. While northern European painters focused on realistic depictions, artists in Italy during the Renaissance favoured images of beauty in idealised portraits. Leonardo da Vinci’s Mona Lisa is an often admired example of Renaissance portraiture.

Paintings served also to educate people about the ancient Classics. Many paintings reproduced legends that most educated people found familiar. Sandro Botticelli’s much-reproduced painting of the Birth of Venus in the 1480s is just one example of a story out of Greek mythology. Other popular topics included Bacchus (Caravaggio, 1596), Narcissus (Caravaggio, 1597–99), Romulus and Remus Suckled by the Wolf (Carracci, 1590) and Venus and Cupid at Vulcan’s Forge (Negretti, 1610).

Northern European paintings also showed changes in social norms. Peter Paul Rubens’s paintings offered vast amounts of naked female flesh at a time when public nudity was largely unacceptable, and yet the churches were among his main customers. The historical context of the paintings mattered. Rubens painted many non-religious scenes as well. Rembrandt painted fewer nudes, but the number of his genuine works is widely disputed and has recently been researched by the Staatliche Museen zu Berlin (2016) and the Netherlands Institute for Art History.

Painting is a technically complex activity that involves more than a good eye and manual skill. The key ingredients to make oil paint are pigment and some form of oil like linseed, and an emulsifier, which can be as simple as beeswax. Basic tools are a mortar and pestle to grind the pigment and spread it, a glass base to mix the ingredients and a scale to measure the amounts. Details matter, such as the consistency of the paste that turns into the base for the paint and the exact choice of pigments so that the colour comes out correctly. Drawing is technically simpler because it involves fewer tools, like charcoal or graphite or a carbon ink, plus a surface to draw on, which can be anything that is moderately absorbent. Etching is more complex because it involves incising an image into a metal plate using acid. Wikipedia (2025z) explains: ‘In traditional pure etching, a metal plate (usually of copper, zinc or steel) is covered with a waxy ground which is resistant to acid. The artist then scratches off the ground with a pointed etching needle where the artist wants a line to appear in the finished piece, exposing the bare metal.’ One use of etching has been in printmaking, which allows the images to be reproduced for a larger audience. Making prints from etchings began in the 16th century in Europe. Printing images using wood blocks was well established in China, but etching did not come to that part of the world until the 17th century. ‘While copper-printing can be traced back to the Yuan Dynasty in China, the art of copper-engraving was introduced by the Italian missionary, Matteo Ripa, in 1711’ (Walravens, 1997).

Painters do not always try to reproduce exact images, and increasingly in the 19th and 20th centuries Western painters wanted to create an ‘impression’ or to give ‘expression’ to ideas they wanted to share. This form of information was not easily reduced to words. The 1884–86 painting by the French artist George Seurat titled A Sunday Afternoon on the Island of La Grande Jatte was meant to create a particular mood that is both tranquil but also intentionally somewhat stiff, as were social relationships at the time. The 1881 painting of Two Sisters (on the Terrace) by Seurat’s contemporary, Pierre-Auguste Renoir, conveys a very different sense of harmony and tranquillity. Pablo Picasso, a Spanish painter who belonged to a somewhat later generation, presented a harsher view of the world, as in his 1937 painting Guernica, which described his feelings about the aerial bombing of the city of Guernica during the Spanish Civil war, a painting that remains vivid even after the greater slaughter through bombing in the following decade. Edward Hopper’s 1942 painting Nighthawks, which shows two lone people at the counter of an all-night New York diner, offers an image of urban loneliness, and Andy Warhol’s 1962 painting of variations of Campbell’s Soup represents a critical commentary on American commercial advertising.

Information in photographs

Photography first became available in 19th-century Europe, after coinciding with a number of other innovations. The ability to project an image on a wall, known as a ‘camera obscura’, was ‘probably known to the Chinese and to ancient Greeks such as Aristotle more than 2,000 years ago. Late in the 16th century, the Italian scientist and writer Giambattista della Porta demonstrated and described in detail the use of a “camera obscura” with a lens’ (Grundberg and Gernsheim, 2025). From the viewpoint of an information scientist, this provided little new information, except insofar as people used the projection to record images in greater detail. The lens made details sharper, but the image was fleeting until a more permanent recording technology became available based on the reaction of silver salts to light, as demonstrated by Johann Heinrich Schulze in the 18th century. A French inventor named Nicéphore Niépce began producing something that could be called a photograph in the 1820s. Louis-Jacques-Mandé Daguerre took the process further by using mercury vapour as a catalyst to speed the development process.

From an information science perspective, the importance of photography grew as its audience broadened to include scholars who wanted to record reactions under conditions where words did not suffice to give a full and detailed account. Early cameras were large and heavy and could not be used for most purposes outside of a studio, but by the time of the American Civil War that had changed substantially. Jeff Rosenheim, who organised an exhibition of American Civil War photography at the Metropolitan Museum in New York, writes: ‘The Civil War created an incredible demand for photography. It was used by the Union and Confederate armies and of course by regular Americans who wanted photographs of their family members heading into danger, and of the battle scenes themselves’ (Hansen and Becker, 2013).

The American Civil War also represents the start of photojournalism. Taking photographs remained a cumbersome operation with cameras that were neither light nor easily transported, and with photographic media that needed careful handling. George Eastman did not develop his highly portable camera and roll film until the 1880s. This technology changed the nature of the information that people could get about distant places or events. Painting and drawing could not compete with the speed and detailed accuracy of cameras. This is one reason why newspapers began to include photographs as early as possible, because they brought an immediacy to the reporting that words could not achieve,

One of the problems with photography was the need to develop the film, which required the right set of chemicals and a ‘dark room’ where the film could develop. Once roll-based film became available, more professionals began developing their own pictures because it gave them control over the final image and because it allowed both editing and control over brightness. Black and white film was easier to develop than colour film, because it required fewer chemicals. Small portable cameras made by manufacturers like Kodak and Agfa created a demand for businesses that would create prints at a moderate price. The popularity of portable cameras meant that middle-class people who could afford a camera and the means to go on a vacation could record new sights and later show them to family and friends. This change enabled people to share information not only about new locations but also about children, pets and friends. The ‘Polaroid Land’ camera appeared in the USA in 1948 and grew in popularity in the 1950s and 1960s as economies improved and people could afford the luxury of instant images. From an information science viewpoint, this was virtually a revolution because it enabled individuals with no special training to capture visual image information almost instantly.

Digital photography represented a further revolution in how people could record information in image form. Early digital cameras were cumbersome and the images they created were larger than most people could easily process with their home computers. Affordable digital cameras did not really become available until the 1990s at the earliest, and even then the quality of the images was significantly less than those from film-based cameras. Early in the 21st century virtually all smart phones could capture digital images. The ability to edit digital images grew simultaneously, and the necessary software became widely available, so that by the early decades of the 21st century everyone could edit pictures. This editing ability also opened up a greater risk of falsification and manipulation. Since digital images were now widely used in scientific experiments as a form of evidence, the ability to manipulate that evidence became a concern for scholarly integrity, and image manipulation has become one of the reasons for the retraction of some academic papers.

Information in theatre and video

Theatre is an ancient art that people have used since the beginning of recorded time to tell stories important to their society, and an important part of those stories includes non-verbal information. For an information scientist, theatre represents a communications tool that is technology independent because it requires nothing more than hearing, vision and the physical presence of an audience. The demands on good actors include the ability to project their voices (which is different than merely speaking loudly) and, ideally, a degree of facial control because expression plays a role in the way people understand the words. Formal theatre can be traced back to the Shang dynasty in China and to the ancient Egyptians in the West. India appears also to have had theatre since the first millennium BCE (Wikipedia, 2025t). In the Western world, Greek theatre has a long and well-respected tradition:

The first plays were performed with just one actor (called a protagonist) and a chorus of people who helped him to tell the story. However, throughout the 5th century BC playwrights continued to innovate. The playwright Aeschylus added a second speaking role, called the antagonist, and reduced the chorus from 50 to 12. His play ‘The Persians’, first performed in 472 BC, is the oldest surviving of all Greek plays.

(PBS, 2024)

The information in these early plays mainly recounted history and religion, presumably with the goal that people would learn the stories and ideas that made up the social context of their cultural past. The Trojan War played a particularly large role in the Greek world, thanks to plays like The Trojan Women by Euripides. Rome also had an active theatrical life. The comic dramatist Plautus was famous in the second century BCE, but many of his works have not survived to modern times. Terence was an equally popular contemporary, many of whose works are available today. Roman theatre tended to follow Greek models, including a chorus and prologue.

Christianity disapproved of the lewdness of Roman theatre and increasingly took measures to discourage attendance. Theatre survived longer in Byzantium, but eventually vanished because of church opposition. Islam did not ban theatre, but regulated it. According to Wikipedia (2025u), ‘The most popular forms of theatre in the medieval Islamic world were puppet theatre (which included hand puppets, shadow plays and marionette productions) and live passion plays known as ta’ziya, where actors re-enact episodes from Muslim history.’ Passion plays also became popular in Christian countries. The city of Oberammergau in Germany has repeated a passion play decennially since the 17th century, and visitors come from a wide range of countries. The point of the passion plays was to remind often illiterate people of notable religious events in the past.

Theatre was already widespread in Europe in early modern times, but its popularity grew as more dedicated theatre buildings were built and as authors like William Shakespeare grew in popularity. Shakespeare’s plays educated the people of his time in histories that were not always quite accurate, but made the events vivid. His plays about British kings gave information about the conflicts that shaped his own Elizabethan age, and served as a legitimation for the Tudor dynasty. French theatre in the 17th century performed a similar educational role. Molière’s plays provide a picture of the social life of 17th-century France and, like Shakespeare’s, continue to be performed. Eighteenth-century German theatre also had historical dramas by writers like Schiller, who, in 1784, described theatre as a means to help the highly splintered German ‘nation’ to unite (Hinderer, 2009). Goethe’s Faust ventured into topics that included religion and philosophy. High schools and universities sometimes include theatrical performances in their activities, not only as a way to teach students about the literature, but to educate them about the past.

Movies did not eliminate theatre, but opened access to drama at prices that more people could afford. Movies became commercially successful even while they were still relatively crude. The 1903 film The Great Train Robbery was an American silent film by Edwin S. Porter produced by the Edison Manufacturing Company, and helped to establish the genre of American ‘Westerns’. Talking movies and colour films established the dominance of film as a means to reach a large number of people, including the newsreels that were at one time a staple of the movie-theatre experience. Television dramas began to replace movies as the way in which most people received stories about new social worlds.
