A comedy like I Love Lucy gave many Americans their first intimate view of life in the apartment world of New York City in the 1950s. Viewers saw the rooms, heard the complaints and learned about managing such buildings from the characters Fred and Ethel Mertz. It was more effective than a sociological treatise – which typical viewers would never have opened.

More recent examples of the power of video come from social media, where smart phones or computers are used to record opinions by people who want to become ‘influencers’ and speak to people of their own age and generation. Sparsh Sadhu (2024) writes: ‘A social media influencer is an expert who has established credibility in a specific industry and has built a large following on social media. By regularly creating insightful and engaging content, creators leverage their influence to promote products, services or causes to their followers.’

Influencers do not necessarily focus only on products, but may also engage in politics. ‘Key to the success of influencers is their ability to convey authenticity. Conceptually, authenticity has different connotations: (a) being true to oneself, (b) being real (vis-à-vis being fake), and (c) being original (Syvertsen & Enli, 2020)’ (Goodwin et al., 2023). The difference between traditional celebrity endorsements and political influencers mainly involves the platform. ‘Though influencers are sometimes perceived as a new phenomenon, their activity follows a history of celebrity endorsements in marketing’ (Goodwin et al., 2023). The messages of influencers involve many non-verbal factors such as tone, facial expression and body language, which play a key role in establishing their ‘authenticity’.

Information in taste and smell

Taste is one of the most hidden sources of information, mainly because no current media can effectively communicate the sensation of taste, except through words, images and sounds. The human ability to smell is surprisingly sensitive. Steven Shapin (2024) wrote in the London Review of Books: ‘It’s said that we can detect as many as a trillion distinct odours, even if we haven’t got the language to describe them.’ There is no way for anyone to get genuine information about taste online. It can be obtained only by putting something onto the tongue or into the mouth. Many people may not consider the information value of taste to be important, but it does matter for anyone who wants to discuss professional cooking, or someone who wants to give information about the actual taste of potentially dangerous foods such as poisonous mushrooms. The same problem exists for the sense of smell, for which modern media have no means for communication. There is a close relationship between taste and smell, as professional cooks know. Video conferencing has become good enough to eliminate some of the need for face-to-face communication, but it is still impossible to share the taste of a meal via video, except indirectly.

The ACM Digital Library describes a research project whose goal is to enable the transfer of taste and smell via the internet, but the success of such experiments depends on special equipment that is currently very rare. Ranasinghe et al. (2011) write: ‘As the initial step, we developed a control system, an actuator, which could digitally stimulate the sense of taste remotely. The system uses two approaches to stimulate taste sensations digitally: the electrical and thermal stimulations on tongue. Primary results suggested that sourness and saltiness are the main sensations that could be evoked through this device.’ The fact that their article was written 14 years ago (at the time of this writing) suggests a lack of progress, which may be due to a lack of funding.

Conclusion

The relative lack of discussion about non-verbal information indicates how frequently scholars ignore it. For information scientists non-verbal information plays a special role because it is context rich, but even information scientists lack any unique vocabulary to describe the role of non-verbal information. The increasing reliance on images and diagrams in scholarly articles is one way that scholars try to circumvent their reliance on words alone. Many journals encourage diagrams, even though they may limit their number, and diagrams rarely play the same role as word counts do. In the mid-20th century, when publishers still relied on paper, diagrams required special handling and special printing, which was expensive. Even after the costs came down, publishers remained cautious about encouraging too many diagrams.

One of the information problems with diagrams in academic papers is that while they show connections between topics effectively, they often lose the detail. The value of many diagrams is precisely that they cut through the verbiage of long explanations, but in some cases they are so complex that they become hard to follow, which weakens their role as an information source. Academic journals could include more pictures or photographs, but scholarly tradition favours text. Electronic journals could also easily include sound recordings, but those are rare.

CHAPTER 13

Information Retrieval for Technology

What is information’s role in creating technology?

All technologies are creations of information. Without retrieving the right information, it is hard to develop viable technologies. Of course, non-technological tools exist. A club lying on the ground in a forest is a simple example. But most modern technologies require active design, involving information. Once a new technology comes into existence it becomes an independent entity that has a role and a context that is different from its components. A simple example is a train, which is a technologically complex travel technology. Most train riders have only a vague idea about what makes it run, and they know even less about how to build one. The core information value of a functioning train is its ability to travel along tracks from one place to another. It is typical of technology that products involve so much complex information that what people understand is only an abstraction of its capabilities.

Technology always has a purpose and context, even though that context may be forgotten once the technology becomes part of everyday life. Technologies can have multiple purposes, depending on the context – sometimes one at their time of creation and another after they become standard. Trousers offer a simple example. Their original purpose was to make it easier to ride horses: ‘Trousers were first invented because robes are uncomfortable to wear on horseback. In fact, those who wore trousers in battle had the upper hand over those who wore robes’ (King and Allen Limited, 2016). Today trousers are not even considered technology but clothing, and until relatively recently as clothing appropriate chiefly for males. Their technological value for riding horses and engaging in battle has faded from popular consciousness.

This means the information that formed a technology shifts over time as circumstances change, as an awareness of the initial purpose of a technology fades. Technologies like chariots, or carriages, or cars replaced the need for riding horses. Information about technology obsolescence takes on a new purpose because it changes the user base, and can serve as an incentive to develop new technologies. The problem is often to get enough information about an old technology to judge which aspects to keep and which to replace.

Reliable information about the limits of technologies is so various that simple explanations cannot always be trusted. Scholars have a tendency to assume that new technologies will create new social conditions that override what went before. The hype about AI offers examples. ‘Expert systems’ had a similar hype some decades ago, and in their time they served a useful purpose for dealing with complex technical matters, without changing the social environments of workplaces. Too often, people imagine science-fiction scenarios where a new technology transforms the world. Of course some science-fiction scenarios do eventually turn into something resembling reality. Space travel became a reality in the 1960s mainly because of its military value, but popular notions from science fiction probably helped to sell the investment costs.

Jules Verne’s stories about space travel made no substantive contribution other than to exploit a well-established interest in exploration, which was important in the social and political context. Verne never tried to explain how space travel could become realistic. The same was true of Ray Bradbury, whose Martian Chronicles continue to inspire an interest in finding life on Mars. Information scientists should know that these fictional works established an interest that contributed indirectly to building a technology for space travel, even while it remained unrealistic. Establishing goals is an important element in new technology development, and the importance of such goals should never be forgotten.

Information retrieval in past technologies

For much of human history sheer necessity drove the need for technological development. Knowing that one is hungry is a form of information, but knowing how to find animals that could be killed and eaten, or knowing which plants satisfy hunger without being poisonous required information retrieval via persons or groups. Necessity probably also led to inventing ways for cutting animal flesh with sharpened stones. Sharpening a stone is not a simple and obvious skill. A person needs to recognise the right kind of stone, and it is a technological skill to learn how to hit the stone at exactly the right angle to make a sharp edge. This is not easy. Retrieving information about where to find the right kind of stones for chipping meant that tribes had to store this knowledge over many years and maintain it within the community. In all likelihood this information was passed along visually and physically by showing others, and perhaps verbally once language was sufficiently developed for this purpose.

Creating fire in order to cook and to stay warm was a life-enhancing technological development whose significance Greek mythology enshrined in the legends of Prometheus, the bringer of fire. Equally important was learning how to keep fires going, which meant finding the right kind of burnable materials, which could include things like dry wood or peat, and learning how to dry them out if they were too moist to begin to burn. It was equally important to learn how to restart a fire after it had gone out, which could mean finding a lightning strike or discovering how to steal fire from neighbours. People also had to invent means to cook meats or vegetables other than just holding them in the flame. Soup was an important new technology that required inventing a watertight vessel that would not break on contact with fire and could hold hot or boiling water for long enough to allow the food to cook.

Similar examples of the use of information to develop technology exist at all times. The development of trains in the 19th century required information that was readily available but needed retrieval because it did not always seem immediately relevant for travel. The early steam-powered trains were technologically complex compared to hitching a horse to a wagon. The information that people needed to begin to develop the locomotive technology grew out of years of experimenting with iron and with steam pressure. Iron-smelting technology had to be capable of producing plates strong enough to handle pressure, which meant assembling metallurgical information. This information had to be combined with information about how to produce high-pressure steam. The developers also needed engineering skills to design boiler plates to contain the steam, and to control the steam to keep the boiler functioning. This may seem simple today, but retrieving and applying the right information to do that was non-trivial at the time. Even knowing how to put metal plates together to contain the steam required discovering information about when to use bolts or to weld the plates. At the time of the first steam locomotive, the social co-ordination of a workforce to complete the complex task was new, and it was necessary to train people to do the work correctly. It is worth remembering that large engineering projects were new when steam engines first appeared.

Technology depends on organisational skills that often took centuries to develop. Historical information about how to co-ordinate working groups has many roots, among them the military. Even the earliest military units required a greater degree of co-operation and co-ordination than was common. The classic technique to enable people to work together was to impose a high level of discipline. Soldiers marching together helped to train men in ways that also had a future social value in factory work. The Prussian military was one of the first to recognise the value not merely of following orders but of having educated soldiers capable of making intelligent decisions even when not under direct orders. Zinkina et al. (2013) write that ‘for Frederick the Great the role of primary education was closely connected with the military needs. Literate officers and soldiers could increase the capability of the army (Melton 1988: 173–175)’. This form of social engineering mattered increasingly over the centuries.

The growth of libraries in the 19th century was symbolic of the need for information in order to support new technologies. The class of British tinkerers who contributed many small inventions to the Industrial Revolution probably did visit libraries, but it may be that the information they needed was available anyway, thanks to others who read books and consulted libraries. The social diffusion of information mattered, even if individual inventors were relatively uneducated. Becker et al. (2011) write:

The main existing evidence on the role of human capital during the Industrial Revolution refers to the technological leader country, Britain, where the established view is that formal education did not play a prominent role in the emergence of new industries (David Mitch 1999). However, surprisingly little attention has been paid to the role of education in the industrial catch-up of the technological follower nations …This paper provides evidence that initially better-educated regions within Prussia responded more successfully to the opportunities created by the outside technological changes from Britain …

Within a laboratory environment, having trained personnel has always been important, and working laboratories often had a nearby library available for the staff in the days before online access. From an information science perspective, the interrelation of information and technology is unmistakable.

Information retrieval for technology today

The role of information in developing modern technology may be clear, but the mechanisms by which developers keep up to date on the information are less transparent. Some people today may assume that everything developers need today is available online, if only they search for it. This is an oversimplification, despite the effectiveness of search engines like Google and Bing which can help in finding online information for those who know how to use them. Very specific and very technical information retrieval searches can be effective when key words are particularly technical, because a high level of specificity makes it less likely that search engines will offer too many false hits, though that does not guarantee that the items the search engines have found are correct. Non-expert searchers risk getting information that seems plausible, but is false or misleading.

Another problem with web-based information for technological development is that paywalls may block important information. For developers at well-funded corporations, a paywall may not matter, but a startup may lack sufficient funds. Start-ups in developing countries may have even greater financial problems in accessing paywall-protected information, which is one reason why information scientists have been active in promoting open access. Some publishers have moved to ‘gold’, ‘diamond’ or ‘platinum’ open access, which means that readers do not have to pay, but authors must, or their institutions or their grant-funders must. Sometimes the advertising revenue pays instead. Open access has significant benefits for anyone doing technical development work, because community members share the articles with colleagues.

One of the unfortunate consequences is that journals have sprung up whose apparent purpose is to get author fees without any serious scholarly reviewing. The result has been that some of the information that they publish is dubious or fake. What makes the situation worse is that simplistic ranking services may use algorithms that give the impression that scholars regard these articles as valuable because they are widely read. Sites like the Retraction Watch blog do an excellent job of flagging the problem articles in these journals, but people developing new technologies do not necessarily take the time or have a desire to check the quality of the information. Many scholars fail to check too, which becomes a problem for information retrieval, when the errors multiply.

Assumptions about language play a role in accessing technological information. A great deal of such information online is in English, and searchers may assume that a search in English suffices. In certain technological areas that may be true, since publishing in English in the natural sciences is almost standard, but searching in only one language opens the risk of information gaps. A complex explanation in English may also include passages that suffer from linguistic unclarity. In a very mathematical field, the unclarity may matter less, so long as the math is correct, but not all technical information is purely mathematical. Information about how to assemble a tool, for example, is likely to be verbal and may not necessarily include diagrams. A search for diagrams can also require specialised vocabulary. For an information scientist, these issues are well known, but developers without search training may get poor results.

Another important information retrieval issue for technological development is access to patent and copyright databases. The patent records for established technologies can serve as a roadmap for designing alternatives that accomplish the same tasks without violating the legal protection. Patents matter, especially for physical devices. Knowing when to file a patent matters. The lawyer Daniel Tysver (2022) writes:

Under current U.S. law (as of the 2011 America Invents Act), patents are awarded to the first inventor that files for patent protection. This differs from the manner in which United States law used to operate, which would award the patent to the individual that first invented the invention, even if they were not the first to file for patent protection.

This means that the inventor needs to know exactly how the new technology will work when filing, but risks not getting the patent by waiting too long to work out extra details. Tysver (2022) advises that ‘[m]any valuable inventions are protected by patents even before the first working prototype is constructed’. The caveat is that the explanation of the product must be ‘in sufficient detail that a person having ordinary skill in your technology is able to create your product “without undue experimentation”’ (Tysver, 2022).

Patent applicants without a legal department to help them must retrieve the legal forms on their own. The rules are different in the EU. People have the choice of registering in only one country or at the European Patent Office, which also requires country-level validation. Nothing is simple. Searching for legal advice may not occur to technology developers, but in a Western society it may be necessary to retain control of the product.

Developers in fields such as computing may choose not to get patents, because the ideas behind the technology matter more than the implementation details. A good programmer can often effectively imitate the effect of lines of code using different but equally effective command sequences. For this reason some programmers prefer to rely on copyright protection that requires no registration. This means that the best way to protect the code-base of a new technology is to keep it hidden, which is also hard because compiled code can be disassembled or reverse-engineered. A quick search can retrieve a number of reverse-engineering tools. That does not mean that reverse-engineering is easy. Assembly language is highly context dependent. It is not just the differences among computing chips, but how the chips interact with the whole machine configuration, including its input–output devices, that can lead to reverse-engineering mistakes. The goal of reverse-engineering is often not to reproduce a product exactly, but to understand more specifically how it works in order to imitate it. For the information scientist, information retrieval is not just about database searching, but about retrieving a range of information that is sometimes almost invisible.

Information retrieval for future technologies

One of the best ways to retrieve information is via a library, with help from trained staff. Digital retrieval works only if the information is on a website or accessible in a database. The ‘dark web’ may also have sources that claim to have information about future and cutting-edge technologies, but it may not be reliable or readily available. Some future-oriented technologies generate a lot of news, such as AI systems, but details about the algorithms and data sources are kept deliberately obscure to protect investments. Other technologies offer less public information and focus mainly on what they do or may be able to do, in order to avoid explaining how they actually work. This means that a developer who wants to retrieve information for future technologies must have a good-enough technology understanding to formulate a plausible search. For someone starting out with little experience, this means a two-step search, where the first step involves background information. Those without natural science or engineering training may find future-oriented information retrieval hard without help.

A logical place to begin an information retrieval search is with public announcements about related technologies. Finding the right technical key words for a search can enable discoveries that give more search terms. Large Language Models offer an example. IBM has a record of providing reliably accurate explanations for readers without broad technical training. On its website IBM writes that ‘LLMs are a class of foundation models, which are trained on enormous amounts of data to provide the foundational capabilities needed to drive multiple use cases and applications, as well as resolve a multitude of tasks. This is in stark contrast to the idea of building and training domain specific models for each of these use cases individually …’ (IBM Corp., 2022).

IBM also provides a link to the phrase ‘foundational models’, which explains that the term comes from the Stanford Institute for Human-Centered Artificial Intelligence and implies something that can ‘be the foundation for many applications of the AI model’ (IBM Corp., 2021). The flaw in this explanation is that it uses the word ‘foundation’ to explain foundational. Readers new to discussions about LLMs may rightly find tautological explanations unenlightening.

The IBM article focuses on the benefits of LLMs and AI systems, rather than discussing how they actually work or the algorithms behind them. The IBM site offers a useful explanation about why earlier systems (including their own) cannot do what LLM systems can. ‘The domain-specific foundation model can be used for many tasks as opposed to the previous technologies that required building models from scratch in each use case’ (IBM Corp., 2023). The article leaves out details that might give away technical secrets, and demonstrates the difficulty in retrieving information about cutting-edge developments. Other searches about LLM systems may give more detailed answers, but those doing such searches need to be able to evaluate the reliability of the results. Information retrieval is no solution for would-be inventors with limited modern mathematical, engineering or other technical backgrounds.

It should be obvious that retrieving cutting-edge information requires the right technical background knowledge, which will vary with the field. In well-established scholarly fields like physics or chemistry, the best sources are still likely to be the leading scholarly journals, conferences, and scholars who are active in both. Nonetheless, there are fields where less academic preparation and formal training is needed. One of the best ways to learn about new developments in such fields is to talk with people at conferences that are open to non-specialists. Of course, discovering the right conferences to attend is itself a challenging information retrieval project.

Once developers are ready to begin working on a new project and believe they have the necessary information, they will likely need information about funding. Ideas and the technical knowledge to execute them are just first steps. Funding sources for technical development break down into several categories. The easiest is of course self-funding, which means having the financial means to begin the work on one’s own, but this is rare for those who do not come from rich families. A rich family offers other advantages in the form of introductions and models for how to approach funders. Connections matter. Elon Musk had the advantage of attending Stanford University, where he could meet more monied people. Information retrieval does little to help with this funding model without the right starting point, but with it, retrieving information about fruitful sources becomes realistic.

Companies with an established interest in technology development and an established willingness to fund outsiders is an option for those who can explain their ideas convincingly. Retrieving information about such companies is easy, because many large corporations like Apple and Google and venture capital firms are open to people with profitable ideas. Getting this kind of support takes a level of verbal persuasiveness that balances technical details with plausible marketing. Finding the right contacts within those companies matters for success. Good MBA programmes can help their graduates make such contacts, but this form of information retrieval relies mainly on personal networks. The criteria that corporations use to fund startups are typically not publicly available information.

Government grants represent a potential source for funding technical development, and a number of countries have special programmes for the kinds of start-up they want to encourage. Some universities have programmes that help start-ups by matching them with academics in technical areas who have experience in getting grant money. Germany has a wide range of programmes for start-ups, which vary from German state to German state. The USA also has many small-business programmes that provide seed-money and some level of management and technical advice. This is an area where online searches can provide genuine practical assistance. Searches for start-ups that successfully used these programmes can be especially useful by providing contacts who can give practical advice about how these programmes work. In the end, connections matter.

Retrieving information about obsolescence

Obsolescence matters even for new technologies, for two reasons. The first is that knowing which technologies are at risk of obsolescence shows where new ideas can find a market share. The second is that understanding the causes of obsolescence can help developers to avoid unnecessary risks.

Computing technologies are among those most at risk, because the field evolves so quickly. For an information scientist, understanding the answer depends both on the context and on the timeframe. Computers have been around for about 80 years, counting from the development of ENIAC (Electronic Numerical Integrator and Computer) in 1945, or slightly longer, since Konrad Zuse developed a computer in 1941, which never went into commercial use because of World War 2. Within the first 20 years after ENIAC (1945–65), two new languages began to compete with programming in the alphanumeric version of native machine languages called assembly languages. The first of these new languages were Fortran (Formula Translation) in 1958 and COBOL (Common Business-Oriented Language) in 1959. They were called third-generation languages, whose processing involved two steps in order to ‘compile’ executable code. Early compilers did not always work perfectly, which meant that assembly language experts occasionally had to fix the output.

These new languages did not render assembly languages obsolete, in part because assembly/machine language remains basic to every sort of computing at the operating system level, but assembly language is essentially obsolete for everyday use, and few computer science students learn it. Both Fortran and COBOL have continued to evolve. Fortran 90 remains important for certain kinds of natural science programming, since it can operate efficiently in a parallel computing environment. In 2021 the ByteScout Team wrote that ‘Many people widely regard COBOL as “dead” or “outdated.” … [But] considering the amount of effort put into the toolchains and the quantity of code currently produced in it, it is far from dead. … However, many businesses are already looking to replace their old code base with new ones.’ Many businesses still rely on COBOL code, in part because the cost of replacing code that works is prohibitive, though this is an area where AI may offer substantial help (see below).

Languages like C or C++ (the object-oriented version of C), Pascal and Python are popular today. The C language dates from the early 1970s and was used in creating the Unix operating system. C is capable of some low-level operations like memory management that third-generation languages like COBOL and Fortran cannot do, but many beginners consider C difficult, because the code is cryptic. Pascal also dates from the early 1970s and is a highly structured language with a formal (and largely inescapable) if-then-else control flow that was meant to enhance readability, but could also seem less flexible to programmers accustomed to other languages. Python is a significantly newer language that appeared in the 1990s. It is an interpreted language, meaning that the Python environment translates the code immediately into executable machine language, which became possible with improvements in processor speed. Python is easy for anyone who knows English to read, thanks to Python’s Dutch developer, Guido van Rossum. Python’s use is currently widespread, especially for beginning programming classes, but it may not be ideal for computationally intensive operations. BASIC (Beginners’ All-purpose Symbolic Instruction Code) is a computing language that had widespread use on early microcomputers, but has lost much of its market share to languages like Python.

Many other computer languages have come and gone. REXX was an interpreted language on IBM Virtual Machine systems and later on other IBM mainframe operating systems. REXX still exists, but has grown obscure. There are too many other computing languages to list or discuss here. Not all programming languages have commands that resemble English, but most appear to use similar command structures. Obsolescence of a computing language can mean that it no longer handles some operations, but more often means that the language’s popularity has waned. AI systems can create effective computer programs. IBM Education wrote:

Programmers enter plain text prompts describing what they want the code to do. Generative AI tools suggest code snippets or full functions, streamlining the coding process by handling repetitive tasks and reducing manual coding. Generative AI can also translate code from one language to another, streamlining code conversion or modernization projects, such as updating legacy applications by transforming COBOL to Java.

(IBM, 2023)

IBM Education (IBM, 2023) also lists three benefits of using AI code generators: it saves time, it helps to test and debug, and it makes coding ‘accessible to non-developers’.

There is nothing new about claims that a new programming tool will be so user friendly that it will replace professional programmers, as some claimed when COBOL first appeared. (Hacker News, 2014) The flaw in these claims is not that newer and more human-friendly languages cannot or have not replaced existing languages, but that every computer program needs proofreading and testing. In that sense, an AI system that creates reliable computer programs is just another higher-level language. In the end, someone (or perhaps another AI) needs to specify what exactly the program should do. Many generations of AI systems have come and gone, and some, like Expert Systems, are no longer considered to be real AI. Obsolescence is endemic in computing technology, and the rate of change may only increase. Developers need information retrieval skills so as to learn what is changing and what is not.

Conclusion

Developers need to consider what information they need in order to create new technologies, and then to think how to retrieve that information. In the past, this might have been as simple as observing what others were doing and thinking about how to improve it. That is not so easy in the modern world, in part because of the complexity of modern technology and the number of people who want to do something similar. Developers need to remember that the key information is not always discoverable in an article or available via a database, because so much depends on the exact context in which a new technology is being developed and exactly what new purpose it will have. Technical details certainly matter, and in the modern world many such details should be available in the scholarly literature, but the key technical information may be secret, and that is unavoidable. The competition over new versions of AI offers an example.

One of the challenges for technology developers is to uncover exactly what information they need, without revealing why there is a balance between the marketing propaganda about what a new tool will be able to accomplish and reliable information about how it will accomplish that purpose. AI developers find themselves in that position at present. There is an enormous amount of speculation about what AI in general can do, but few developers want to expose specific algorithms for specific applications to a competitive public. A related example is the sensors that allow for self-driving cars, which means that developers need to retrieve both AI and engineering information. The sensors need sufficient sensitivity and sophistication to recognise environmental changes quickly, since that affects what the car needs to do. A sensor must, for example, be able to distinguish between a small child who suddenly dashes out into the street and a ball whose size may seem to resemble the head of a child. Simply slamming on the car’s brakes if a child is in danger makes sense, but braking too suddenly for a ball could cause an accident if a second car were too close behind. AI alone is not sufficient to allow autonomous driving, because the AI needs to be able to perceive the whole complex environment around the car, and to make decisions based on that. That is a reason why self-driving cars are not allowed on all streets in all countries at present.

CHAPTER 14

Information Curation

What is information curation?

Information curation involves preserving information and making it usable over time. Curation is arguably as old as the first written records, which would make clay tablets the earliest curation medium, and some of those clay tablets have lasted to present times. Oral traditions also helped to curate information. In the ancient world, poetry was a preservation mechanism because the formal structure of poetry made it easier for people to remember long strings of words. The Iliad is an example of this form of curation, since it survived in purely oral form for generations. Chinese poetry has an equally ancient tradition. Curation often involves both organisation and access in addition to simple preservation, and the earliest libraries provided a greater degree of curation than had existed previously, even though they lacked clear policies and the technical expertise to preserve the storage media (papyrus or vellum or, later, paper). All they could do was to put content in a safe location, which was itself a non-trivial contribution.

Librarians have traditionally emphasised systematic organisation as an element of curation, and such access depends on rules about filing and locating works reliably over time. Today these rules are generally part of cataloguing or classification systems. In the past, libraries sometimes just kept materials in accession order with sequence numbers often based on the date of acquisition. One of the hallmarks of a good librarian in past centuries was the ability to find (often simply to remember) where a particular work was on a shelf. In a small collection, memory might suffice, but as collections grew, that ceased to work. Curation as a principle applies to all forms of information, not just words on paper, and it is equally important in museums and archives. Curating museum objects represents a particularly challenging task because of their non-verbal nature, since most classification systems depend on some form of written description. This leads to descriptive metadata for museums that is long and complex and less standardised than that of libraries, since the information objects are less uniform. Archives should, in theory, have fewer challenges in creating reliable organisation schemes, but the contents of archives are sometimes so idiosyncratic that outsiders find them intransparent.

Digital curation is newer because computers are newer. Digital curation has in some sense been around since the first computer operators began to store data on external media such as magnetic tape in the 1950s. Tape was a flawed medium because it was too vulnerable to physical damage, especially when being loaded onto a machine by an inexperienced operator, but tape was at least readily available and was arguably more reliable than punched cards or using disk storage, which was in the early years both unreliable and prohibitively expensive. Cassette tapes instead of open-reel tapes became more common in the 1990s and largely replaced reel-to-reel tape readers, not only because of their greater reliability but because loading them took less skill. In recent decades other forms of digital storage have emerged, including optical disk. The reliability of storage media has increased over recent decades, but media is only part of the consideration. All media deteriorates eventually, and the important question for information scientists is how to make sure that the information survives when the medium breaks down.

In their 2016 book, Gillian Oliver and Ross Harvey cite Daisy Abbott from the University of Glasgow for her concise definition of digital curation: ‘the management and preservation of digital material to ensure accessibility over the long-term’ (Abbott, 2008). The definition is true, but ignores the urgency. Victoria Reich, one of the founders of the LOCKSS project (Lots of Copies Keep Stuff Safe), expressed the urgency in 2012: ‘If you don’t preserve digital content then it won’t exist. Most of society’s culture and commercial assets are now digital but, generally, the move from print to electronic is about access rather than preservation’ (Reich and Kiefer, 2012). Her point is clear: effective digital preservation is essential for passing the current cultural heritage on to other generations, otherwise it will not exist. There are people who believe that paper copies are the best form of curation, but even if that were true of written works, it would not suffice for the full range of contemporary digital information that needs curation and preservation.

Pre-1800 curation

The pre-modern world was never perfectly successful in preserving texts or objects, but some incomplete information has come down to us today. Curation efforts in the pre-modern world were not systematic, and were often limited to things with a clear economic value at the time, which meant that many popular tales and everyday playthings have been lost. Insofar as they were preserved, it was mainly by chance. China and parts of the Middle East may have preserved more information content, but that is hard to measure, since the whole pre-modern world suffered from wars, invasions, conquests and levels of violence that did not respect any form of information. The Christian West and the Islamic Middle East cared about preserving sacred religious texts and relics, and China had a proud literary tradition that gave an incentive for preserving a wide range of literary works, but no one knows how much was lost. From an information science perspective the problem was the lack of any concept of systematic curation or physical mechanism for saving information that was not immediately necessary.

Curating information was a luxury in pre-modern times, when people had few resources for anything except the necessities of everyday life, but many Christians viewed sacred texts as a necessity for their afterlife. Copyists in monasteries contributed materially to saving a wealth of information that had only tangential religious value, including some legends that could be treated as cases of divine intervention. Historians often credit the monasteries for their efforts to preserve manuscripts. The sheer number of monasteries meant the creation of multiple copies, which was also important for longer-term preservation, on the same principle of LOCKSS (Lots of Copies Keep Stuff Safe) (see below).

Error rates were a problem. The monastic workers made plenty of writing errors and often used abbreviations in order to avoid writing out full words. Literacy levels among the copiers were highly variable. Many copyists had a limited knowledge of Latin, and may not have understood all of the words they were writing. Proofreading was at best irregular, and there was no clean way to replace false words or incorrect spellings after they were discovered. Storage for the texts often meant putting the pages on a desk or shelf for later binding. The codex form of books existed and had replaced scrolls by the fourth century CE in the West, but scrolls had a longer life in China. One problem with scrolls was their relative fragility as compared to the solid mass of a codex, and people sometimes compensated for this by storing them in protective cases. Some media were also more robust than others. Vellum made from animal hides was expensive, but did not easily deteriorate. The types of ink varied too, with a wide range of recipes for the pigments and the materials used to bind the pigments, such as gum arabic or other resins.

The art world feels strongly about preserving the original, but text preservation is more focused on content preservation via copies. Printing matters for content curation, both in Europe and China, because it radically increased the number of copies of any given work. Printing did nothing to improve the curation of museum content, but the increasing wealth of society made a difference because more people could buy art and other image-based forms of information, and any increase in numbers helps preservation. If the goal of preservation is saving the information, not just the object, copies of museum works also matter.

In the 16th century Pieter Breugel the Elder painted many scenes of peasant life. Among the most famous were his paintings The Wedding Dance (1566, now in the Detroit Institute of Arts) and Dutch Proverbs (1559, now in the Berlin Gemäldegalerie). These paintings preserved social information not otherwise easily available, and the popularity of his works meant that others imitated him, including his sons. Italian Renaissance paintings were often rich in background detail, even though they gave less attention to the life of the poor.

One of the advantages of oil paintings was their relative robustness over time, because the colours did not fade and the canvas or wood base tended to survive. Frescoes were more vulnerable, depending on the environmental conditions. The frescoes of Pompeii survived the eruption of Vesuvius in 79 CE, thanks to the volcanic ash that covered the city. They depict details of everyday life, including fruit, fish, vases, banquets and scrolls as well as mythological scenes, and are one of the richest sources of social information about first-century Rome. The fact that this information survived to modern times was due in large part to its long burial and slow rediscovery by professionals wanting to preserve it.

Buildings serve also as lasting information sources. Many ancient sports arenas have left archaeological records because they were so solidly built that they could be used for other purposes. The amphitheatre at Arles, France, is an example: ‘In the Middle Ages, the monument served as a home and refuge for the people during troubled times. The ancient amphitheater was transformed into a real walled and fortified city with 212 houses, streets, squares and even churches. The walls of the amphitheater were used as defensive ramparts during the sieges led by the barbarians’ (Arles Tourism Board, 2024). The surviving baths and villas of Roman Britain provide information about heating systems, hygiene and places to wash. The same can be said of medieval castles and palaces, which survived better than peasant huts. What historians know today about peasant life comes mostly from archaeological investigations.

Throughout the era before 1800 systematic attempts at curation mainly involved things of religious value. This was also broadly true for all cultures and regions, with the exception of certain kinds of official documents, treaties or deeds, and even they could be lost or forgotten. Preserving access worked only when interest in doing so was high enough, but discovery aids such as metadata, insofar as they existed, were local and themselves needed curation.

Curation: 1700 to the computer age

Libraries and museums are key to information curation. Libraries existed well before 1700, but tended to be private and local. An exception is the Bibliothèque nationale de France, which grew from the 900 volumes of King Charles V in the Louvre palace in 1368. King Francis I guaranteed the library’s growth by requiring legal deposit for all printed works at the Château de Blois, a collection that moved to Paris in the mid-17th century (Bibliothèque nationale de France, 2024). Many other libraries became established in roughly the same period. For example, the Bavarian court library dates from 1571 and began benefiting from legal deposit in 1663. The collection expanded significantly in 1803, when it acquired the Bavarian cloister libraries (Bayerische Staatsbibliothek, 2024). The British Museum (which housed the British Library) was founded in 1753 and quickly grew into a repository for information of every sort, thanks to the wealth of the British Empire. At about the same time the Prussian government established the institution that became the Prussian State Library (today the Staatsbibliothek zu Berlin): ‘As of 1701 it was known as the Königliche Bibliothek (Royal Library) and was the court library of the absolute ruler. The ruler regarded this institution as his own private library – accessible to the public …’ (Staatsbibliothek zu Berlin, 2025b). Other Western European libraries also grew during the centuries before the French Revolution. Substantial libraries also existed in China from the time of the Shang dynasty, but remained largely private collections until modern times.

The first librarian at the Bibliothèque nationale de France entered into office in 1719. His name was Abbé Bignon and he was ‘appointed librarian to the King in 1719, [and] gave the library unprecedented brilliance. He organized it into departments: Printed Material, Manuscripts, Medals and Engraved Stones, Engraved Plates and Collections of Prints, Titles and Genealogies. He also continued the work of his predecessors in terms of acquiring documents, very concerned with bringing in all the important works of learned Europe’ (Bibliothèque nationale de France, 2024).

From an information science perspective collecting and organising the physical materials that contain information is part of curation, and contributes indirectly to preservation because it prevents their loss. The abstract intellectual task of separating intellectual content from its physical carrier took longer, in part because of a lingering sense that the original object had a special economic value. First editions of notable works still continue to fetch high prices. Physically reproducing a printed work grew much easier because of the printing press, which meant accurate copies of intellectual contents became more widely available.

An important question is what responsibilities Abbé Bignon undertook beyond collection building. There is evidence that he created five departments that separated printed works from manuscripts and other information sources. In information science terms that separation was intellectually important because the information contents of manuscripts are often different even if the book began originally as a manuscript. At the time of Abbé Bignon many documents were only in handwritten form, such as treaties and legal agreements between two parties. Museums especially needed to focus on curating physical objects, because there was no tool for making copies. Until top museums are ready to accept digital copies as adequate, they have many of the same curation problems in the digital age that they had in the 19th century,

Organising, classifying and protecting museum works was all that most curation efforts could do, because the problems were greater for museums than for libraries due to the range of physical materials as well as the intellectual complexity of the contents, for which verbal descriptions were often inadequate. The profession of museum curator was also slower to develop the kinds of standards that have become common for librarians. There is no specific degree requirement for museum curators comparable to those for librarians. University degrees in history or art history are common, but the field is less regulated and often less well paid, though that varies by institution and country. In the pre-digital world, curating the information in museum objects depended on physical preservation, combined with detailed descriptions and photographs for situations when the object had deteriorated beyond repair or (more commonly) was stolen. The range of physical materials in museums is broader than for books and manuscripts, and the effect of aging could mean that in order to address preservation needs museum curators need to understand the chemistry involved in long-term environmental effects for a wide range of materials. Different types of wood age at different rates, and humidity is often a factor. Metal is relatively robust, but will also interact with a range of environmental factors over time so that it discolours or becomes brittle. Physical curation has no simple solutions.

Museums and libraries in the 19th century took curation seriously, but could only curate the materials they had on hand. World War 2 involved serious looting and physical damage to buildings from bombing. War zones around the world have seen even more deliberate damage. The aerial bombing of Coventry Cathedral in 1940 largely destroyed the 14th-century cathedral and its treasures. Despite the best efforts of the community, ‘it was clear that little had been saved’ (Historic England, 2024). The firebombing of Dresden in 1945 not only destroyed the cathedral but destroyed large portions of the inner city: ‘Before World War II, Dresden was called “Florence on the Elbe” and was considered one of the world’s most beautiful cities because of its architecture and art treasures’ (Britannica, 2025f). A few European cities, including Rome and Paris, escaped major destruction. Post-war Berlin and London showed the scale of the war damage for decades afterwards. No curation efforts can protect against this kind of deliberate violence, and the best that anyone at the time could do was to remove anything capable of being moved, such as the art collections in both London and Berlin. However, the Berlin collections were not safe from theft by invading soldiers.

Major European wars offer only one example of the problem. During the Cambodian civil war in the early 1970s, most of the ancient temples of Angkor Wat survived thanks to collaborative efforts between the armies, but restoration work had to stop (News CNE, 2021).

Camping Khmer Rouge forces used whatever wood remained in the building structures for firewood, and a shoot-out between Khmer Rouge and Vietnamese forces caused a few bullet holes in bas reliefs. In the late 1980s and early 1990s, far more damage was done to the reliefs by art thieves working out of Thailand who lopped off many sculptures and other structures, including earlier reconstructions.

(Wikipedia, 2025v)

The efforts to save famous artefacts from notable sites should not be undervalued, even though they are often later forgotten. Perhaps the most common curation problem is simply negligence. For example, students often throw away notes from lectures, which is generally no problem, since such notes may neither be complete nor completely accurate, but such ephemeral information can have significant informational value.

In the winter of 1830 the later Utrecht professor in Economics Jan Ackerdijck arrived in Berlin, where he followed lectures by Hegel, the most famous philosopher of his time. In Ackerdijck’s diary we read that he met Hegel several times, and had his lecture notes on Hegel’s first series of lectures copied in a notebook.

(Utrecht University, 2022)

[Hegel’s] notes were subjected to perpetual revisions and additions. It is possible to form an idea of them from the shape in which they appear in his published writings. Those on Aesthetics, on the Philosophy of Religion, on the Philosophy of History, and on the History of Philosophy have been published by his editors, mainly from the notes of his students …

(Knox, 2024)

Philosophy is not the only case where lecture notes became the basis for a significant publication. Based on the lectures and the tape recordings, a team of physicists and graduate students put together a manuscript that would become The Feynman Lectures on Physics (Wikipedia, 2025w). The content of these lectures was challenging:

Caltech’s educators aim high. They wanted to stop using textbooks as the primary material for the first two years of college physics and instead teach it through a series of original lectures … Audio (but not video) recordings were made, and other faculty transcribed the lectures, intending to edit them into a new textbook. Things did not go as planned. The editing process was grueling and required, as the editors reported in their foreword, ‘the close attention of a professional physicist for from 10 to 20 hours per lecture’. Even then, the editors were dissatisfied with the ‘completeness, smoothness, or logical organization’ of their product. But they decided to publish anyway.

(Crease, 2014)

The point is that curation does not only involve finished products, but can involve intermediaries with a significance of their own.

Librarians and museum staff generally do their best to curate materials by preserving them and by making them accessible. Microfilm was seen as a preservation option in the mid-20th century, but microfilm was neither user friendly nor entirely error free, as users discovered.

Digital curation

Digital storage was long seen as risky, and for good reasons. At the beginning of the digital era, the early mechanisms for preserving digital information were more fragile than for paper. Typical offline digital storage solutions at the time were punched cards or reel-to-reel magnetic tape, but cardboard punched cards had the same weakness as any paper products. The card decks lacked any structure to keep them in order. Punched cards were popular for data entry until companies like IBM developed online systems like TSO (Time Sharing Option) in the early 1970s that allowed programmers to enter lines of code directly without relying on punched card operators, but many companies had invested in punched card typing pools. The process was also time consuming. A programmer had to write the code on a paper form, which then went to the data-entry department. Typically, the punched cards came back to the programmer, who then needed to get them to a card-reader device to load into the computer. If the programmer discovered an error, then the process had to be repeated.

Each company had its own way to preserve card decks, and that depended on filing systems ill designed for retrieval. Often they looked like oversized card catalogue drawers, but with little to indicate what the individual cards meant. Professional punched card typists were efficient for general data-entry tasks, but the quantity of punched cards quickly became a storage problem, in part because containers for the cards were poorly adapted to storing corporate-level quantities, and were even more poorly designed for retrieval. Punched cards presented a space problem too, since the containers took up significantly more room than reel-to-reel tapes. Many companies that relied on large amounts of data gave up on punched cards relatively early.

Storing data long term on ‘spinning discs’ was simply too costly until the 1990s, when the reliability of computer discs improved and the costs went down. As a general rule, organisations only kept data on spinning disks when they needed to have it readily accessible. In the early days there were removable discs, which operators could put on a shelf and retrieve when necessary. These removable disks were somewhat less expensive, but were themselves a storage problem and had vulnerabilities. Later versions of spinning discs were faster, did not allow removal and were generally more reliable. Operating systems determined where data was stored on the discs. Data was generally not stored sequentially. Instead, the operating system stored data on particular tracks of a disk and set up a reference to know what data could be found where. This means when a disc ‘crashed’ it could be possible to reconstruct some of the missing data by searching through undamaged tracks on the disk. As a rule this was done only in emergencies, but it was possible when the data were so valuable that recovering them was worth the trouble.

Serious long-term digital preservation and curation began in the late 1990s and early 2000s. The basic approach built on converting paper-based texts into digital images and using the newer generation of reliable discs to store content and to make it accessible. By this time reliability presented fewer problems than legal issues like copyright, which introduced the additional complication that copyright laws differ somewhat from one country to another. Storage itself eventually became less of an issue than formats, because the format affected usability. In simple terms, an image provided less accessibility than machine-readable content, but the reliability of optical character scanning processes left something to be desired, especially in the early years. Some form of human correction was often necessary. However, the important first step was to make sure that text-based content was in digital form.

Most of the current digital long-term archiving solutions grew out of the years between 1996 and 2004. Three countries made active contributions: the USA, Germany and the UK, and the leaders of these efforts quickly came to know each other, or were already in contact. The first project was the Internet Archive, which Brewster Kahle founded in 1996 after successfully selling his WAIS system. One of the key focuses for the Internet Archive has long been to harvest web content, much of which could vanish completely. The Internet Archive uses its own harvesting tool called Heretrix and a tool called Alexa. Heretrix respects flags that indicate a site does not want to be crawled, which is important for copyright reasons, and it stores the harvested data in WARC (Web Archive) files. The Wayback Machine provides access to Internet Archive content.

In 1997 the German Research Foundation (Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft) funded two major digitisation projects, one at Göttingen University and the other at the Bavarian State Library. Those libraries had a substantial number of important works, including a Gutenberg Bible in Göttingen and a wealth of medieval manuscripts at the Bavarian State library. Visiting scholars used both collections heavily, and digitising content made them much more available to the scholarly world. Only shortly thereafter, Peter Schirmbacher implemented Humboldt Universität zu Berlin’s edoc-Server in 1999, which was the first institutional repository. The edoc-Server did not harvest content, but was available for people with appropriate authorisation to store academic content, including doctoral dissertations, journals and other kinds of publications. The edoc-Server never sought to be a universal solution to digital archiving and curation, but provided an example of how to manage digital content and make that content freely available.

When the US National Science Foundation’s ‘Digital Library Initiative’ project went into its second phase, one of the projects that it funded in 1999 was ‘Lots of Copies Keep Stuff Safe’ (LOCKSS) by Dr David Rosenthal and Victoria Reich of Stanford University. As the name suggests, content was scanned and stored on servers in multiple locations in order to ensure sufficient redundant capacity in case of disasters or system failures. The users of member libraries always have access to content in LOCKSS. This project was explicitly intended for long-term digital preservation and access, and it involves a number of networks in different locations, including the LOCKSS global network and various private LOCKSS networks, including one in Canada. CLOCKSS (Controlled LOCKSS) also began officially in 1999. The CLOCKSS website describes the service as ‘Digital content is stored in the CLOCKSS archive with no user access unless a “trigger” event occurs’ (CLOCKSS, 2024).

JSTOR did not begin as a long-term archiving project, but as a means for freeing physical storage in libraries. The goal was to

convert printed scholarly journals into electronic form and store them in a centralized digital archive. Participating libraries and their institutions could free physical space, reduce capital and other costs associated with collection storage, and vastly improve access to scholarly research.… In 1995, following a pilot launched under the direction of the University of Michigan, JSTOR was established as an independent not-for-profit organization.

(JSTOR, 2024)

The Michigan staff realised that digitisation was also a way to preserve the contents, and launched Portico in 2002 as a tool for the copyright-compliant preservation of digital content. Initially, Portico offered contracts to publishers that excluded recent issues of journals. The length of the exclusion period varied over time and became shorter as publishers discovered that it did not affect their sales. Portico has been part of the Mellon Foundation-funded ITHAKA organisation since 2005 (Portico, 2024). Since Portico focuses almost exclusively on English-language publications, Elmar Mittler at Göttingen University established ‘DigiZeitschriften: das deutsche digitale Zeitschriftarchiv’ [the German digital journal archive], which serves a purpose similar to Portico for scholarly journals in German.

Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT) and HP Labs unveiled the DSpace institutional repository in 2002, three years after the edoc-Server began operating in Berlin, but with far more publicity. DSpace was meant to be sold to other institutions that would want to customise it. DSpace secured a long list of academic and commercial customers around the world, thanks to active marketing. This meant that digital curation became almost mainstream. Other digitisation projects followed, most notably the Google Books project, which began in 2004. The University of Michigan and Stanford University libraries were the primary initial partners, with contributions also from Harvard, New York Public Library and the Bodleian Library at Oxford University and, after 2007, the Bavarian State Library. Michigan insisted that partner libraries should receive digital copies of the works and persuaded Google to accept higher quality standards for the scans and underlying text. In 2008 management of the digital copies in the libraries shifted to a new entity called HathiTrust, under the leadership of the Committee on Institutional Cooperation (which included Michigan) and the libraries of the University of California system.

There is at present no entity that actively co-ordinates this broad range of digital creation projects. The projects are in general loosely in touch with each other, in part because the community working on long-term digital creation is relatively small. Participants from the various projects talk with each other via a wide range of academic organisations including the US-based Coalition for Networked Information and the Digital Creation Centre at the University of Edinburgh.

Conclusion

Information curation has seen considerable progress in recent decades as many have accepted the fact that preserving only the physical original does not suffice, and that for text-based information a digital copy provides better access than physical copies. Digital representations of physical information sources have also become more standard. Digital imagery can capture three-dimensional works in enough detail to be a viewing option, even if viewers prefer the original. Digital music has not replaced the enjoyment of a live concert, but has effectively replaced the earlier physical means of recording music, such as long-playing records.

For the information scientist better training in long-term preservation remains a priority. Information still remains fragile, perhaps for the old reason that people forget about everything not immediately urgent. Every information source requires active curation, and costs are a factor. Justifying the cost is hard for those who see no immediate value. The question arises: how much redundancy is necessary? LOCKSS typically uses seven well-separated servers to guarantee the safety of digital content because the statistical likelihood of all seven failing at once is low. People often feel that a physical copy is more authentic than a digital one, which makes less sense for text than for physical objects. Museums have discovered that people still want to see the original painting, even if high-quality digital reproductions are available on the internet. For many people, digital solutions have not eliminated the need to curate physical originals.

CHAPTER 15

Information for Education

What is information for education?

Education is about imparting information to prepare younger people for their working life and their ongoing role in society. The difference between formal education and informal education depends on the social organisation of schooling and the availability of other learning opportunities. Separating information from education is practically impossible. Children begin learning from birth, and one of the first things they learn is their parents’ language, which lets them communicate their needs and enables them to understand adults. A substantial literature exists on all stages of education, including the very earliest stages between parents and children, and those very early years may be the most important for later information acquisition. Early education is a topic that information science programmes have rarely emphasised, but recent research has taken an interest in the information behaviour of young children (Schlebbe, 2023).

There is ongoing interest in how older persons learn to make use of online information (Hillebrand, 2024). For the information scientist, looking at how people learn cannot be confined to early years or school-based training. Anyone who has had jobs involving modern technology must be aware that the rate of change is high, and learning new information represents an essential part of work life. The same is true for every kind of job, except perhaps completely mechanical ones under close supervision. People working as waiters in restaurants must, for example, interact regularly with new technologies to take orders and to accept payments.

The trouble is that new information arises constantly, so people must reeducate themselves. Learning is not identical with education, but learning is an educational goal that is often measured formally. Education is not exclusively academic or abstract. People who learn how to drive a car not only need information about how the vehicle operates and about the laws governing driving; they need to also acquire physical skills for steering, braking, shifting gears (on cars without automatic transmission), parking and, perhaps above all, the skill to remain aware of the surroundings. These physical skills are all forms of information and need refreshing over time because circumstances change.

For the information scientist, an important issue is how different the kinds of learning are. Language learning offers an example because it varies substantially from country to country and person to person. There is evidence that ‘A child’s brain seems to benefit from growing up multilingually. Advantages are not only seen in language skills but also in other areas. Several studies have shown that children who grow up speaking more than one language are more likely to put themselves in someone else’s position’ (NeuroNation, 2017).

The physical effects are also noted: ‘Brain imaging studies in bilingualism using various tasks have suggested that bilinguals may employ at least some different brain regions depending on the language used in the task’ (Kobayashi et al., 2008).

There is still much that no one really understands about how the brain processes information.

Education before 1700

Education before 1700 in Europe was mainly a private matter. It was somewhat more formal in China at the time: ‘The roots of a system of formal education in China can be traced back at least as far as the 16th century B.C. later Shang Dynasty (1523–1027 B.C.) Throughout this period education was the privilege of the elite few, and for the most part existed for no other purpose than to produce government officials’ (Surowski, 2000).

Entry into the bureaucracy depended on passing rigorous exams. The students could prepare for these examinations based largely on the Chinese classics by enrolling in the private institutions ‘which existed for no other reason than to prepare students for the civil service examinations’ (Surowski, 2000). This was probably the earliest form of required education and it emphasised both humanities topics and legal learning. The examination system lasted for centuries. It was required in the sense that the information needed to pass examinations was formalised to a degree that may have discouraged learning at home.

The closest Roman counterpart was training in rhetoric, for which there were formal schools such as Augustine described in his Confessions. Rhetoric was important both for a political career and for law. It was not general education, any more than was the Chinese examination system. It is unclear in the pre-Christian era how much learning mechanisms became formalised into what one might call schools, but learning was certainly needed for those working with law. The fact is that people had to learn to read and write in order to discuss political and philosophical issues. Family members were not the only teachers. The wealthy could hire tutors (Greek slaves were popular tutors for a time in the Roman Empire), and older children could contribute. Education focused largely on males, but women in well-to-do families frequently learned along with their brothers. Many sources emphasise that the main goal of education for women was to prepare them for marriage and for running a household, which needed some level of literacy: ‘some important literature written by women during this period has survived, and many other women writers published work that has not been preserved’ (Plant, 2004). Plant lists 55 known female writers during the Roman period. It is hard to say how many works by women were lost because no one felt they were important enough to save.

The situation for women in ancient China was similar. Wang, van Leeuwen and Li (2020) wrote that ‘women were given the opportunity to learn to read and write’, though they note that education may have promoted obedience as a female virtue. The authors note, however, that ‘[i]t was not uncommon that, when a male who earned the money to support his family was unable to work due to disability or had died, educated women would either run the business by themselves or generate other income by selling their paintings or calligraphy, or serving as private teachers in wealthy households’ (Wang, van Leeuwen and Li, 2020). Literacy in ancient China, as in pre-modern Europe, was low even among the elite, but that does not mean that women did not find ways of educating themselves in areas that mattered. Running a household and feeding a family required a broad range of information-based skills, even for the illiterate or barely literate, and it is entirely possible that (mostly male) professional historians have underrated how much practical and abstract information women learned.

The situation in India was somewhat different. Yasin and Hina (2023) write that even though the ‘primary duty of women was to take care of the home and children’, ancient Hindu sources ‘strengthen the claim that the women in ancient India were educated, intelligent and an important part of the society’. Religious books formed the basis of education in India, as in Europe. Yasin and Hina (2023) make the argument that ‘As far as general education among Hindus is concerned, the education system of ancient India was unique in its nature and aims. Not only philosophical subjects were taught there but general subjects like literature (religious and secular) arts and sciences were also taught.’

Little reliable factual information exists about women’s education in the ancient and pre-modern world, presumably because it took place outside of official notice, and mostly must be extrapolated from the writings of women themselves, insofar as any survive.

Formal education in Europe existed mainly in the context of the Church, which needed moderately literate clergy, and people to support them. Even leaders like Charlemagne wanted a scholar to help with official documents. His most famous assistant was Alcuin, who grew up in the ninth century in Northumbria and attended a church school at York, where he learned the trivium, which included grammar, logic and rhetoric, and the quadrivium, which included arithmetic, geometry, music and astronomy, which played a long-term role in medieval education. Charlemagne may have learned to read, but likely he had people read to him and dictated written communications. The evidence is unclear, but it is clear that his court attracted people who were literate, that he respected them and that they played important roles in running the empire.

A group of students founded the first medieval university at Bologna in 1088, and hired scholars to teach them, which is a reverse of the current practice. It is not completely certain that Bologna was the first university: Charles Homer Haskins (1957) notes that Salerno may have had an earlier medical school, but Bologna and, two years later, Padua were among the earliest to resemble a modern university in teaching a range of subjects. The students had organisations like the guilds that gave them a measure of power. Haskins (1957) also explains the origin of professors and examinations:

Excluded from the ‘universities’ of students, the professors also formed a gild or ‘college,’ requiring for admission thereto certain qualifications which were ascertained by examination, so that no student could enter save by the gild’s consent. And, as much as ability to teach a subject is a good test of knowing it, the student came to seek the professor’s license as a certificate of attainment, regardless of his future career. This certificate, the license to teach (licentia docendi), thus became the earliest form of academic degree.

Paris was different: ‘Paris, in contrast to Bologna, was a university of masters’ and it received a royal charter in 1200 (Haskins, 1957). Paris also set up different faculties, each with a dean, which is an ecclesiastical title that has remained in active use.

The next-earliest European university was probably Oxford. There is general agreement that its foundation by Alfred the Great in the tenth century was likely apocryphal. ‘There is no clear date of foundation, but teaching existed at Oxford in some form in 1096 and developed rapidly from 1167, when Henry II banned English students from attending the University of Paris’ (Oxford University, 2014). Those early students went to Oxford, where there was already a church school. Cambridge University was founded only slightly later in 1209, probably by students from Oxford. For an information scientist it matters that young people who wanted to learn appear to have taken the initiative in starting these universities. The information that students at these early institutions acquired mattered for their careers, especially in law or theology, and did not so clearly represent the same social or family pressures that are common in modern times.

Other universities also soon came into being. For example, Charles University in Prague ‘was founded in 1348 during the rule of King of Bohemia and Holy Roman Emperor Charles IV. It became the first Studium generale north of the Alps and east of Paris. Modelled on universities in Bologna and Paris it quickly gained international renown. Originally, it had four faculties: theology, liberal arts, law, and medicine’ (Charles University, 2024). The university soon faced an era of religious upheaval, in part because ‘the religious reformer Master Jan Hus was rector of the university at the time’ (Charles University, 2024). The language of the university was German until 1882, when the Czech National Revival led to dividing it into a Czech and German university.
